How to read this report

The catalog follows the order Ashley Hamilton gave these documents. Each numbered folder in Martha M. Hamilton Documents is catalogued as one document, and the image files inside it are that document's pages, in sequence. Folders appear in numeric order; pages Ashley left loose in the folder root are grouped as Unfiled, and the handful of scanned pages that do not appear in her ordered set are grouped as Not filed, each with its reason recorded. Both groups come last.

Each document carries an order basis saying how much the page sequence can be trusted: not-asserted (14) · content-sequence (6) · internal-pagination (4) · editorial-chronological (3) · explicit (1) · editorial-alphabetical (1). Only explicit and internal-pagination mean the order is written on the pages themselves; not-asserted means the grouping is Ashley's but the sequence within it is ours. The basis is recorded per page, because a folder is often only partly evidenced: where a page's basis is weaker than its document's label, the page says so in its own provenance line. A document rated mixed order has some firmly evidenced pages and some that are not — check the pages before trusting the sequence.

The transcriptions themselves were produced by reading each photograph with human-style visual inspection — no OCR software was used. Where the images are separated by blank or damaged "table surface" photos, those separators are preserved in place as table-photo pages.

Separately from the order, every document carries a reading confidence rating (high / medium / low) describing how reliably its text could be transcribed; a multi-page document is rated by its least legible page. Passages that could not be read are marked [illegible] rather than guessed, because accuracy matters more than completeness for archival material. Text that is rotated sideways is noted in the transcript.

Where pages contain columnar data — salary tables, newsroom headcounts, pay gaps — the values are rendered as tables to preserve the rows and figures. Click “View images” on any document to see the original scans, and expand a document to read its full transcription.

DOC-001
Martha 1

Draft letters and statements on the Juan Williams case, 1991

letter editorial-chronological medium order medium read 1991 3 pages

Subject: Martha Hamilton's typescript drafts responding to The Washington Post's handling of the Juan Williams sexual-harassment complaint, including a letter to Ben Bradlee Jr.

Notes: Three related 1991 drafts about the same episode. Ordered by the dates written on the pages (Oct 2 -> undated final page -> Nov 1). Page 2 of the Bradlee letter (DOC-040) was not filed by Ashley and appears in the Not filed group.

p. 1 content 3208105C-C6AF-4BD4-AF34-6C3C45DB856A_1_105_c.jpeg
[pencil annotation, top right: 5] [red pen annotation: Oct 2 1991]
This is submitted as either a "Taking Exception" piece or as a Letter To the Editor.
As female staff members of The Washington Post we were both surprised and angered by Richard Harwood's Ombudsman column last Sunday, "Dirty Talk." In trying to defend the behavior of our colleague Juan Williams, Harwood reveals himself to be ignorant of both the actual circumstances surrounding "the Juan Williams affair," as he calls it, and sexual harassment in general.
Harwood correctly notes that the Washington Post newsroom is a sexually diverse one. He misses the point, however, when he implies that its diversity should protect male staff from complaints of sexual harassment, and that the complaints against Williams arose because of some irrational and sudden intolerance on the part of some female staff members.
Sexual harassment is not simply sexually explicit language. It is verbal abuse that happens to be sexually oriented, comments that are meant to hurt, belittle, humiliate, threaten or embarrass another person, thereby making the newsroom a hostile environment in which to work.
The complaints against Mr. Williams were not the first sexual harassment case in The Post's newsroom, as Harwood should know, nor were they the first complaints about Mr. Williams' behavior, as Harwood should have discovered before he wrote his article. Female staff ~~members~~ have complained to their ~~supervisors~~ about Williams' harassing behavior for at least four years. Nor did only one woman in the newsroom complain on this occasion. More than a dozen women complained about ~~Juan Williams~~ and a number of men expressed outrage at his behavior too.
The fact that Williams is, as Harwood states, "a family man...whose professional career...is on the ascent" is beside the point. "Family men" do engage in offensive behavior. Furthermore, many of the women who were harassed by Williams are family women.
Harwood seems to believe that many of the complaints against Williams stemmed from outrage over his piece on the Post op-ed page defending Clarence Thomas and that the women bringing the complaints were victims of some sort of mass hysteria perpetrated by an identification with Anita Hill. This notion simply flies in the face of the facts.
Not only was Williams aware that problems with his behavior were longstanding, but the most recent series of complaints against him occurred before the world had ever heard the name "Anita Hill." Williams was made aware of the investigation into those recent complaints one day before his piece defending Thomas ran. In fact, Williams was given the option to withdraw the piece. He did not.
What Williams actually wrote about Thomas in that article is not the issue here. More significant to us is that Williams, knowing he was under investigation for making sexually harassing comments, spoke about the issue on television during the Thomas confirmation process, when, ethically, he should have recused himself from such discussions. At the very least, we believe he had an obligation to disclose his personal involvement with the subject to the readers of his op-ed piece and to his viewers, who might have regarded his opinions differently had they understood the context in which his comments on Judge Thomas were made.
Williams himself has contributed to the media hysteria around this case, first, by not recusing himself from the issue and withdrawing his editorial page piece, then, by granting myriad press interviews in which he denied all charges
[margin annotations at right of paragraphs 5-6: a brace and a diagonal line with initials, possibly "MW"]
[struck words above shown with strikethroughs as visible on the page]
p. 2 partial 2ADEBF18-5451-4049-A2EA-CA3050A9CF2A_1_105_c.jpeg
[page begins mid-sentence:]
...and grossly misrepresented the kind of comments he was accused of making. For example, in an October 15, 1991 Washington Post story describing the controversy, Williams asserted that "the Post seems to have labored mightily to produce some charges against me and they have come up with a big zero." Thereby calling into question not only the credibility of his accusers, but of the newspaper. The most recent example of this kind of statement was the October 30, 1991 USA Today article in which Williams, having agreed to confidentiality as part of the resolution of the Post's internal inquiry, nonetheless asserted falsely, that the Post has apologized to him for the situation, thereby implying that he had been vindicated. "Come back to work. We're sorry this happened" was his characterization. He further stated, as is not the case, that the comments that offended his accusers were totally innocent inquiries. "Hi, how are you?...remembering a spouse's name..."Hey, did you have a date?" Unsuprisingly, media commentators, such as Larry King, have concluded that Williams was falsely charged and has been exonerated.
Finally, had his defense of Thomas not become a paradigm of responsible journalism to Republican members of the Senate Judiciary Committee, who held ^his article up on television and read it into the record, this ^incident might well have been handled as the women involved wanted: as an internal personnel problem with a colleague, remedied quickly, privately and with dignity.
[handwritten annotation to the right of the last paragraph:] his editorial
Jodie Allen, Jeanne Cooper, Kelly Doe, Barbara Feinman, Nancy McKeon, Jeanne McManus, JoEllen Murphy, Deborah Needleman, Amanda Spake, Kathleen Stanley, Karen Tanaka
p. 3 content 71B00413-C2B0-473C-9070-29D43A637CBB_1_105_c.jpeg
[handwritten at top] letter drafted by Martha Hamilton
Nell Henderson and others
Nov. 1, 1991
Dear Len,
We, the undersigned, want to express our continued concern over the way newsroom management has handled the repeated sexual harassment complaints by almost two dozen Post employees (male and female) over at least four years against Post writer Juan Williams.
Although we feel sympathy for Juan's family and understand the Post's policy of maintaining the confidentiality of personnel actions, we feel Juan's unrebuted [sic] false statements to the national media continue to cause anguish and professional harm to the women involved. They also have left many people inside and outside the Post with the impression that either the complaints were not serious or were not taken seriously. This continues to harm the Post as an institution and raises concerns for all of us who have a right to work in an environment free of discrimination and hostility.
This case illustrates the inadequacy of the Post's handling of sexual harassment complaints. The Post needs to take stronger steps to send a clear and unequivocal message to everyone here that such behavior will not be tolerated. We recommend that the Post's policy be improved in the following ways:
• The policy of confidentiality, while appropriate in most cases, is ill-suited in the case of an individual making false public comments that are injurious to his colleagues.
The Post has an obligation to set the record straight by refuting such comments. The Post could publicly state simply that its inquiry determined that the complaints were of substance, were substantiated and led to disciplinary action.
The Post need not publicly specify the complaints, the names of the complainants, or the action.
• When complaints are verified, the Post should ask the victims how the matter can be resolved in a way that will restore their sense of safety and comfort in the workplace.
• Management also owes the victims a clear statement on how the matter was resolved.
• Lower-level supervisors should pass such complaints up the chain-of-command, rather than allowing them to fester, as they did in this case, for four years before reaching the North Wall.
We appreciate your attention to our concerns and understand your reluctance to further publicize a matter that continues to hurt the Post and many of our colleagues. However we feel the best way to lay it to rest is to confront it openly and honestly.

-Post Unit News-
Washington/Baltimore Newspaper Guild
No. 99 Date October 22, 1987
Discrimination at The Post
PAY GAPS WIDEN
The patterns of pay discrimination that have contributed over the years to low morale and wide gaps in the amount Washington Post managers are willing to pay their employees not only persist, but have widened significantly since management imposed its version of the contract in August.
If you needed a reason to understand why this contract is inadequate, here it is.
Last we looked, white males topped the salary scales in the best-paying jobs in many of the largest job categories at the Post. The news is this: despite the company's stated commitments to the hiring and nurturing of women and minorities, the practice of paying inequitably based on race and sex has not only continued--it's gotten worse.
For reporters--the largest single job category covered by the contract--the average salary for white males was $988.68 at the beginning of 1986. By September of 1987, that figure had jumped to $1,140.52.
Such a salary improvement is not the source of our complaints, however. The problem is that while white males were continuing to improve, females and blacks continued the slide away from parity.
The numbers compiled after the so-called impasse was declared in mid-August tell the story.
Across all classifications, white males average $332 more a week than black females, $204.39 more than white females and $230.66 more than black males.
Among reporters, the figures are reversed slightly, but the discrepancies remain just as stark. White male reporters, on the average, make $240.59 more a week than black females, a gap that is $43.24 wider than in January of 1986.
The gap has also grown by about $2 a week between white male and white female reporters and by about $15 a week between white male and black male reporters.
Among outside advertising salespersons, the gap also increased significantly. White males, on average, earned about $25 more a week than black males and black females in 1986, a disparity which has now increased to $96 weekly. White females in this classification earn on average $61.51 less than their male counterparts, up from a $43.03 average weekly difference in 1986.
And the gap among white men and white women who work as T-5 Data Programmers has opened dramatically, moving from an $8.33 average weekly difference in 1986 to an $86.07 average difference now.
There have been some improvements. Black females who are classified as managers, assistant managers, editors, deputy and associate editors have closed a more than $200 gap between their average salaries and salaries for white males.
But overall, the Post's record on equal pay for equal work sends a clear message to the rank and file at the Washington Post about which employees are valued at this company, and which are not.
These comparisons tell the story:
Reporters 1/86 9/87

DOC-003
Martha 3

Union newsletter pages asking whether the Post discriminates

arbitration-exhibit not-asserted medium order medium read mid-1980s 3 pages

Subject: Bargaining-committee and Guild newsletter pages comparing Post salaries with the New York Times and with starting salaries by race and sex.

Notes: Thematic grouping of three newsletter pages. Order not asserted. Includes page 2 of the Guild flyer whose page 1 is in Martha 2, and page 2 of DOC-030 whose table photograph (page 1) is in the Not filed group.

p. 1 content 9A931AA6-0F20-481B-AD27-66A079CEFE99_1_105_c.jpeg
[handwritten top right: DATE?]
Let's see, now. It's not longevity.
It's not experience. It's not discrimination ... Oh, I get it.
Blacks and women aren't as good as white males.
Post publisher Donald Graham has said that longevity at the Post and experience are major factors accounting for disparities in pay between white males and others. But that's not what The Post's own pay data show. In the newsroom, where management has the most flexibility in setting pay, long service at the Post and experience count less for women and blacks than for white males, who often command higher salaries even with less experience and fewer years' service.
The Post's pay data show a gap in salaries for reporters (the largest group of workers represented by the Guild) that has gotten bigger since dual pay began. White male reporters and white female reporters hired before the multi-tier pay system began make roughly comparable salaries today. Not so for those who were hired after dual pay became a reality.
And that discrepancy isn't explained by differences in experience either, the data suggest. For instance, the median salary for white male reporters hired during 1980-1984 is $190 a week more than it is for white female colleagues hired during the same time period. But both groups are comparable in age, which suggests that they probably differ little in experience.
Although the pattern appears to be better pay for white males than for blacks or women, not all white males are treated equally. For instance, younger, less experienced new white male hires are often paid considerably more than more experienced Post veterans who are also white male.
Let's go to the statistics:
White male reporters and white female reporters hired during the five years before the 1975 strike at The Washington Post and the beginning of the dual pay system here make roughly comparable salaries. The median salary for white male reporters hired in 1970-1974 is $24.90 higher than the median salary for white women reporters hired during the same period. Only two black reporters were hired during that five year period and are still on the payroll.
In October 1975, the press operators at the Post went on strike, an event that divided the Guild at the Post-and led to a challenge to the Guild's right to represent workers here. While that challenge was pending, our contract expired and the Post refused to negotiate a new one until the issue of whether the Guild was the bonafide bargaining representative for Post employees was resolved.
HOW MUCH MORE IS A WHITE MALE REPORTER WORTH?
[Bar chart: 'HIRED' with three grouped bars for hire periods]
1970-1974
1975-1979
1980-1984
Axis 0 25 50 75 100 125 150 175 $200
[bars are unlabelled in the copy aside from the axis; legend text not legible in this image]
Dual pay begins. And the salary gap grows.
In the meantime the Post began handing out across-the-board pay increases of 5 and 6 percent to workers who were already here. At the sam. time, the Post was hiring new workers at the outdated contractual minimums-or slightly above them. In other words, new employees were hired at rates that didn't reflect the pay increases that everyone else had received. That was the beginning of dual pay.
Some new hires were treated more generously than others. The median salary for white male reporters hired during the next five years is $145.27 higher than the median salary for white female reporters hired during the same period. It is $36.09 higher than for black male reporters hired during the same period and $241.12 higher than for black female reporters.
p. 2 content D337D813-A483-4D97-B30C-31E90B1EA787_1_105_c.jpeg
Could it be that the Post discriminates?
Post management says no. Post management says, in effect, that if women and blacks are good enough — and old enough and experienced enough, they will command the same salaries as white males. (Publisher Graham asserted to a bargaining committee member that minorities command premiums, although the data don't seem to bear that out.) If so, should we conclude that women and blacks who are not commanding comparable salaries are not good enough? Are the many white males who also suffer from an unfair and arbitrary salary system also failing to measure up?
We don't buy that. We don't think management can get by with blaming the victims.
The culprit is dual pay, which keeps minimums so low that the subjective decisions made in setting salaries and awarding merit increases are allowed to carve out a discriminatory pattern. The Guild's pay proposal, which would phase out dual pay at the top of the scale over three years, would reduce the gaps in pay based on race and sex considerably by the end of this contract. Handing out merit pay increases won't do it. Father knows best won't do it.
Equal pay for equal work will.
Still with us?
Of the top 25 earners in the newsroom, one is black and two are female. That's 88 percent white males. Of the top 100, seven are black and 13 are female. And, just in case you wondered, there is no overlap: there are no black women in the top 25 or the top 100. The median age of this group is 43. In other words, half of the group is 43 or younger, so it's not just venerable white men, either.
We have focused on reporters because reporters are the largest group of workers in the Guild unit and also because more merit pay is dished out in the newsroom, so salaries there reflect management discretion more than in some other areas of the newspaper.
[Chart:] OUTSIDE SALES
Y axis: MEDIAN SALARIES ($600 to $1,000). Legend: WHITE MALES (solid bars), WHITE FEMALES (hatched bars). X axis: HIRED — cohorts 60-64, 65-69, 70-74, 75-79, 80-84. Each cohort shows a taller white-male bar and a shorter white-female bar (gaps widening for the 60-64 and 75-79/80-84 cohorts; values roughly $920/$840, $870/$730, $880/$730, $790/$800, $780/$700 respectively — approximate from chart).
[Chart:] COPY EDITORS
Y axis: MEDIAN SALARIES ($0 to $1,000). Legend: WHITE MALES, WHITE FEMALES. X axis: HIRED — cohorts 60-64, 65-69, 70-74, 75-79, 80-84, 85+. White-male bars consistently at or above white-female bars (near parity in 60-64 through 70-74 at about $1,000/$990, $960/$950, $960/$950; then $900/$880, $850/$790, $780/$750 approximately — read from chart).
The charts above show the median salaries for different groups of employes. We have chosen two of the larger categories and also categories where the number of women and minorities hired is large enough to illustrate whether or not there are disparities in pay. Although there are some deviations from the pattern, by and large white males seem to be better paid than women or blacks hired during the same time frame. But another pattern is clear, too: Dual pay is driving most salaries down.
-- Martha Hamilton, for the
Bargaining Committee
p. 3 content 5E0626DD-A23F-4939-A99F-3C3B7A47B86A_1_105_c.jpeg
Dual pay was incorporated in the contract.
And the gap grows wider.
On July 30, 1979, the Post and the union reached agreement on a contract to replace the one that had expired more than three years before. The Post demanded a dual pay system as the price of agreeing to a contract, and dual pay became the rule. The top minimum salary for fully experienced reporters got relatively lower each year, compared with what the minimum was at similar papers such as The New York Times and compared with what reporters who had worked here for several years were earning.
In time the top minimum got so low that the Post was given a free hand in setting salaries. The Post top minimum is $664.35 today compared with $929.60 at The New York Times and compared with the $841.46 minimum that a reporter who was fully experienced and on the payroll here as of March 31, 1976 must be paid.
After dual pay went into effect formally at The Post, the gap in salaries between white male reporters and white female reporters grew greater. The median salary for white males hired during 1980-1984 is $190.16 higher than the median salary for white women reporters hired during the same period. Only five black reporters were hired during that five year period who are still on the payroll.
Management's answer isn't borne out by the facts.
When Donald Graham made a recent radio appearance to defuse criticism that the Post was racist, he was asked about pay disparities here.
"To begin with, reporters at The Washington Post now average slightly over $50,000 a year," he said. "...We're talking about variations among people making pretty good money. Within that, due to some historical circumstances at the Post, on average, not in every case, but on average, the people who have been at the newspaper a longer time make more than people who have been there less."
That's not what the data suggest.
The median salary for white male reporters hired during 1980-1984 ($954) is about the same as the median salary ($955.58) for white female reporters hired more than a decade before during 1965-1969. It is substantially higher than the median salary for black males hired during 1965-1969. The median salary for those veteran black male reporters is $45.11 less than the more recent white male hires.
Longevity and greater experience also don't necessarily count for much for white males. The median salary for white males hired 20 years earlier in 1960 - 1964, is only $39.45 more than the median for white males reporters hired in 1980 - 1984 and $35.81 less than for white males reporters hired in 1975 - 1979.
Another argument that has been made to account for the difference in pay is that the Post is hiring older, more experienced white males.
Based on the birth dates of those reporters hired during 1980-1984, the median age of the white males was 34; the median age of the white women was 33, and the median age of the black reporter was 41 [struck/garbled: "II. It appears"] unlikely that the white males were significantly more experienced than the other hires.
CHART: REPORTERS (legend: WHITE MALES, BLACK MALES, WHITE FEMALES, BLACK FEMALES; y-axis MEDIAN SALARIES from 600 to $1,100; x-axis HIRED in cohorts 55-59, 60-64, 65-69, 70-74, 75-79, 80-84, 85; grouped bar chart, values not individually labeled)
Newsroom management has cited "merit and the marketplace" as important determinants of what individuals are paid. The Post seems to be comfortable ratifying the discrimination that prevails in the marketplace. As a union, we're not. We think that women and minorities should be able to walk into the Post and command the same salaries as a white male with comparable experience walking into the Post.

DOC-004
Martha 4

'It Doesn't Pay to Be Female or Black' and 'Why does the Post discriminate?'

newspaper-clipping not-asserted medium order high read 1986-1999 2 pages

Subject: Two illustrated Guild / bargaining-committee pages comparing 1982 and 1986 salary data by race and sex.

Notes: Thematic pairing of two salary-comparison graphics pages. Order not asserted.

p. 1 content 705804F1-ED54-4239-9128-961FAB0857DC_1_105_c.jpeg
[handwritten, top right] DATE?
At The Washington Post
It Doesn't Pay to Be Female or Black
Five years ago, the Washington Post company settled a sex discrimination case brought against it by the Guild by agreeing to hire more women. The company reported to the federal government recently that it has more than met its hiring goals in every category, and in every department.
What it did not tell the government is that it is paying those women, as well as its black male employees, less than it pays white men.
In the best paying jobs and in many of the largest job categories, the Washington Post pays white men significantly higher salaries on average than white women or blacks doing the same jobs. This is pay discrimination.
Patterns of pay discrimination that existed four years ago not only persist, but in some cases have worsened with the gap between the salaries of white males and others growing wider.
In the case of reporters — the largest single job category covered by the contract — the average salary for white men is $988.68. Black men average $920.46; white women reporters average $859.37, and black women reporters average salaries of $791.33.
Although in some jobs the gap has narrowed between the salaries of white males and black males, women, especially black women, have been left further and further behind.
In the case of reporters, for instance, white women reporters were paid 11.2 percent less on average than their white male colleagues in 1982; now they are paid 13.1 percent less. In 1982, black female reporters were paid 15.8 percent less than their white male counterparts. Four years later, they are paid 20 percent less than their white male co-workers. In 1982, black men were paid 14.7 percent below on average than white male reporters; in 1986, they have narrowed the gap and are paid 7 percent less.
In 1982, in the category of assistant editors, assignment editors and makeup editors, white women were paid 5.5 percent less well than white males in those jobs. That gap has grown to 8.6 percent in 1986. In 1982, all blacks (9 of them) were paid 4.9 percent less than their white male colleagues. Now there are 10 blacks in this category, and nothing has changed: They make 4.8 percent less than their white male counterparts.
Those two categories alone account for 277 — or about a quarter — of the fulltime employes covered by the current Guild contract.
There have been some improvements in the past for years, however. In outside advertising sales, white males average $809.21 in weekly salary. White females average $766.18. Average weekly pay for all blacks is $784.24. (To break down the data for the nine blacks in the department by sex might indicate individual salaries.)
Disparities in pay were worse in that department in 1982. Then white female employes received salaries 18 percent lower than the salaries of their white male colleagues. Now the gap is only 5.3 percent. Then blacks received salaries 4.3 percent lower than their white male colleagues. Now the gap is 3.1 percent.
In some departments dominated by women or by blacks (generally the lower paying jobs), white males often are not the best paid workers. In telephone sales, for instance, black women command the highest salaries.
[chart, partially cut off at bottom of page] REPORTERS — HOW OTHER SALARIES COMPARE TO WHITE MALE AVERAGE SALARIES, 1982 & 1986. ASSISTANT EDITORS,

HOW OTHER SALARIES COMPARE TO WHITE MALE AVERAGE SALARIES, 1982 & 1986
[Chart: y-axis 'WHAT WHITE MALES AVERAGE' from -2 to -10; panel WHITE FEMALES: bars -5.5% (1982), -8.6% (1986); panel ALL BLACKS: bars -4.9% (1982), -4.8% (1986)]
WHY DOES THE POST DISCRIMINATE?
We don't think that the Post has a conscious policy of discrimination but it discriminates.
We don't think most managers and supervisors at the Post discriminate consciously on the basis of race and sex. But conscious or unconscious, the results are the same.
The salary system that the Post created and maintains allows discrimination to do its work.
A major cause of the discrimination in pay is the multi-tier wage system. The system began in the late 1970s when there was no contract in effect. During that time and the Post unilaterally handed out pay increases. When the Post finally settled on a contract three years later, those pay increases were not incorporated in the contractual rates of pay. As a result, people who had been on the payroll before the contract was signed had higher salaries than those who were hired later. That pattern was extended — at the Post's insistence — in the contract signed in 1979 and in the current contract.
In the late 1960s and early 1970s, blacks and women faced another kind of discrimination. They could not get hired for the better paying jobs here. The Post has made progress since then in hiring more women and minorities. But it also set up a pay system that favors employees who worked at the Post before 1976. Among reporters and editors, outside advertising sales staff, top-ranked computer programmers and in other better paying jobs, that means white males. Since then the Post has hired an increasing number of blacks and women — but at lower salaries.
But that's not the only way in which The Post discriminates. To try to determine whether the patterns in salaries were entirely the effect of differences in longevity which favor white males, we looked at the salaries of all fully experienced reporters hired in a single contract year within the past two years—all hired as "journeymen" reporters, all hired by the Post at approximately the same time.
The results? Fully experienced white males hired that year now are paid an average of $1046.68. Black males hired that year and rated as fully experienced now average $950.89. White female reporters hired that year as fully experienced average $817.47. There were no black women hired that year who were rated as "fully experienced" under the contract. But then, there are only eight black female reporters out of a total reporting staff of 191, according to the Post's statistics.
If minimum salaries set by the contract were higher, it would result in more pay equity and in less freedom for the Post to set salaries arbitrarily — a freedom that has resulted in discrimination. At the New York Times, the key minimum is $929. At Newsday, it is $1,000. At the Washington Post it is a low $664.35, and if you are a black or a woman in a commercial or editorial job that earns the key minimum, your salary is likely to be much closer to that minimum than if you are a white male.
IF THE WASHINGTON POST IS SERIOUS ABOUT ELIMINATING DISCRIMINATION AT THE POST, IT WILL MOVE AWAY FROM THE MULTI-TIER SALARY SYSTEM.
— Martha Hamilton, for the Bargaining Committee
OUTSIDE ADVERTISING SALES
HOW OTHER SALARIES COMPARE TO WHITE MALE AVERAGE SALARIES, 1982 & 1986

IN THE MATTER OF MARTHA HAMILTON,
CAROLINE MAYER, CYNTHIA L. SKRZYCKI,
SHARON W. WALSH AND THE WASHINGTON POST
Declaration of Martha McNeil Hamilton
I, Martha McNeil Hamilton, declare as follows:
1. My work experience prior to coming to the Post¹ included the following:
Reporter, The Newark News, 1966
Reporter, Freelance and stringer for Copley News Service, North American Newspaper Alliance and Time Magazine from Buenos Aires, 1966-67 which included, among other things, doing a series for Copley on the Antarctic
Writer and Assistant Editor, The Machinist, weekly, 1968
Reporter covering agriculture, oil and the Supreme Court, The New York Journal of Commerce, 1969-70
Assistant to the Dean and Acting Director of Admission, LBJ School of Public Affairs, University of Texas
Assistant Director, Agribusiness Accountability Project (public advocacy and research organization). In this job I also wrote articles for The New Republic and the Texas Observer
Author, The Great American Grain Robbery, published by the Agribusiness Accountability Project in 1972
2. I had an application pending at the Post for many months but hadn't heard from the Post until I received a call on a Sunday (I would learn later that this was just after the women in the news room had filed a sex
1 I have a B.A. in history, with a minor in classics from the University of Texas.
p. 2 content 8EF47E16-55A7-4B7F-96D2-BDA8F91778E7_1_105_c.jpeg
discrimination complaint), asking me to come in for an interview.
3. Then AME Metro Editor Harry Rosenfeld offered me a lower salary than I was earning and told me, "I'm not saying to you, take it or leave it. I'm saying, please take it." The salary offered me was at the 3rd year experience level under the collective bargaining agreement. Because I had at least 3 years experience as a reporter and writer, I attempted to negotiate a higher salary, i.e., at the 4th year experience level. The Post would not agree. I began work in July 1972 at a weekly rate of $210 (or $10,920 per year)--$57 a week less than I was making previously.² I believe that men hired at about the same time were given increases to come to the Post and were not asked to take pay cuts.
4. My first job at the Post was as a city staff reporter, general assignments³ in the Metro Section. As a city reporter, I got off to a fast start and was assigned to Capital Hill to cover D.C. and metro area legislation. I covered the passage of home rule which came during a period when the newspaper was preoccupied with Watergate. Home rule passed Congress in 1973.
5. My next assignment was to cover Montgomery County, where I worked for Maryland editor Herbert Denton. Herb got along poorly with several reporters, myself included. Herb wanted me off his staff, as I learned in a meeting with Len Downie (then AME Metro) and Herb in which they tried to persuade me to quit.
6. Len Downie called me in and told me that they thought I wasn't right for the job and put me on night
² The Post's pay records reveal a $13 weekly rollback in pay from $210/wk to $197/wk on 9/17/72, two months after I began. The records reveal a "springback" in pay of $5 weekly on June 7, 1973. I have no explanation for this apparent cut in pay.
³ A general assignments ("GA") reporter has no beat but is available to help out on a variety of stories.
- 2 -
p. 3 content 32386A42-EEB5-4A2D-996E-EEFE0151BF4D_1_105_c.jpeg
[handwritten signature across the top margin; not legible as a name transcription]
police (generally viewed as the least desirable beat on the city staff), reporting to City Editor Kevin Klose. I told Kevin that Herb and Len were wrong about my abilities and that I would prove it, if he gave me a chance. For many months, I worked night police and worked during the day as well. In time, I was getting on the front page consistently. Klose told me he was very pleased with my work. He took me off of night police and gave me the D.C. schools beat during a period of enormous controversy. I was on the front page very frequently.
7. Then, in October, 1975, the Post strike by its press operators occurred. I did not cross the picket line to go to work for several months until the strike was over. After the strike, I came back to work and was consistently on the front page.
8. In May 1976, on the day my daughter was born, I left for maternity leave, finishing a front page story a few days after my daughter was born on May 27, 1976.
9. I had one month's paid leave as per the collective bargaining agreement and four months leave without pay. I also asked for two weeks sick leave following the birth of my daughter. I was denied that, though my doctor said I would be physically unable to work for two weeks after giving birth. I filed a grievance which subsequently was settled by the Post making a payment to me. Later, as a member of the bargaining committee I succeeded in clarifying in the contract that sick leave could be used after the birth of a child which is now standard practice with many women being paid 8 to 10 weeks sick leave.
10. When I was preparing to return to work after five months maternity leave, I came in to talk to Len Downie with a three-page memo about story and beat ideas. But Downie short-circuited my pitch, telling me that he had decided to put me on the Weekly staff so I could spend more time with my baby. This occurred even though I had been writing major front-page stories in the months before I gave birth (and a few days after I gave birth). I told Len that I didn't want to go to the Weekly and asked him to reconsider, but he didn't.
- 3 -
p. 4 content 10049231-6CAB-4133-A703-D3AC0A1A2D20_1_105_c.jpeg
11. I was side-tracked on the Weekly for many months, but ultimately came up with a project on the proposed convention center and then a series on developer Dominic Antonelli that got me on the front page. The series won the Front Page investigative reporting award for that year (1978). Downie, who was AME Metro, gave co-author Karlyn Barker and me bonuses for the series. My bonus was not incorporated into my base pay as a merit increase would have been. Downie told me he would take me off the Weekly. However, to open up a slot for me, he had to tell Bart Barnes that he was being sent back to Sports.
12. This apparently was hard for Len to do, so I stayed on the Weekly staff for several weeks until he managed to do so. I became a roving investigative reporter in Metro just as Len was leaving the Metro section. He was replaced as Metro AME by Tom Wilkinson. I spent many months on an investigation that included getting access to sealed court records, proving that former Rep. Joel T. Broyhill, a long-time friend of parking interests, had been set up in a no-show job by two parking magnates after his retirement, with benefits including a Rolls Royce Silver Cloud. Wilkinson said he didn't want to publish any of this because Broyhill, at the time, was John Warner's campaign manager and Wilkinson thought it would reflect unfairly on Warner.
13. In approximately 1979, Wilkinson was replaced as Metro AME by Bob Woodward. I didn't really have an assignment editor to whom I reported. I wasn't being used very well and had a hard time getting anyone interested in my projects. For instance, Woodward took interest in one project only after Bill Greider in Outlook expressed an interest in publishing it in Outlook. So I started to look for a different position. I was awarded a National Endowment For the Humanities fellowship to study economics in 1979 and was required to take leave without pay during the fellowship.
13. I began talks with Frank Swoboda, then day editor of Financial, about going to Financial. I went there on a temporary basis in October 1979 and quickly began doing front page work, writing about subjects including the national economy, budget issues, the environment and consumer debt.
- 4 -
p. 5 content 8FED28BA-BD84-4AB2-9FDD-F96DB7E924FE_1_105_c.jpeg
14. Financial AME Pete Silberner hired me permanently for Financial (in April 1980). I covered beats including computers, the Securities and Exchange Commission and the tax-writing committees on Capitol Hill.
15. AME Frank Swoboda asked me to serve as editor of Washington Business, and I did beginning in January 1985. Prior to this, the Deputy AME Jerry Knight had been editing Washington Business. For that promotion, I received a $50 weekly pay increase (or approx. 5.75%) pursuant to the collective bargaining agreement. In this position, I served as editor of Washington Business (a Monday tabloid) and served as the assignment editor, directing local business coverage, including local financial coverage of the savings and local scandal.
16. During 1986, I was replaced as Washington Business Editor by Don Brazeal, who had recently come to the Post as a copy editor. Swoboda told me that he was not displacing me because of any issues with the job I was doing, but because Len Downie, then managing editor, wanted Brazeal in an assignment editor job. In 1995, Brazeal suddenly left Digital Ink, the Post's online operation, to "go on sabbatical" from which he never returned.
17. Subsequent to leaving the Washington Business and daily editing post at Swoboda's request, I served as editor of the Sunday Business Section. Swoboda described my new assignment in a personnel announcement as being "the nanny" for the section. After a short period, I asked to go back to reporting, because editing this section was less than a full-time job.
18. In 1986, I was a Washington Post reporter-in-residence fellow at Duke University for a month.
19. A sense of the environment in the newsroom during this period is exemplified by the fact that during this period women and minorities challenged the fact that when an important figure such as the head of the Federal Reserve Board or the head of the New York Stock Exchange was invited to lunch at the Post, women seldom were invited, even when they covered the guest of the industry in
- 5 -

question. Instead, male reporters who had nothing to do with the subject matter at hand sat down with the guest and top editors. This practice changed after Jane Seaberry and others brought it to Swoboda's attention.
20. In 1986, a report was issued by a Post task force which focused on recruitment, hiring, retention and advancement of blacks in the Post's newsroom, and which surveyed newsroom employees. Page 7 of that report stated "[t]hroughout the newsroom, sexism is seen as more prevalent than racism." See Declaration of Rick Ehrmann at Tab D.
21. In early 1987, I returned to work as a reporter covering the airline industry. According to my pay records at the Post, my salary was reduced about $47 per week at the same time--even though I understand that the practice is not to reduce editors' salaries when they return to working as reporters. Because pay records are not available to me, I do not know whether the men who moved from editor to reporter positions received a reduction in pay.
22. I covered the airline industry from 1987 until 1993. I covered the aftermath of airline deregulation, including the emergence of new carriers such as Presidential Airlines and the Pan Am Shuttle; the decline in airline service and on-time performance; the bankruptcies of major carriers such as Continental Airlines, Eastern Air Lines, and Pan American World Airways and the role of corporate airline executives including American Airlines Robert Crandall, Donald Trump and Frank Lorenzo. I was frequently on the front page. For instance, in 1989 I had 18 front page stories. And I regularly beat the competition, often covering -- either by myself or with Frank Swoboda (who had returned after his stint as AME) -- the same stories that the Wall Street Journal and the New York Times covered with a larger number of reporters. During this period, I produced frequent consumer service stories, including explainers on the airlines' increasingly sophisticated pricing of each airline seat and the hazards of overhead storage in aircraft cabins.
23. I received merit increases when Pete Silberman and Swoboda were AMEs. However, during most of the period when I was doing the high-profile coverage of the airline
- 6 -

industry, often working 6 or 7 days a week and staying on the story in bankruptcy court in New York regularly to 2 or 3 a.m., Peter Behr was AME and, in that roughly five year period, Behr only gave me one merit increase -- in Nov. 1990. During the same period, I have reason to believe a number of men got more merit increases.
23. While I was covering the airline industry, I also was covering oil and energy. In the late 1980's AME Behr took me off of the oil and energy beat. The reason was not related to my performance; instead, he said he had been asked to do so in order that Tom Lipippmann, who was returning to the National staff as a reporter, could cover the beat for National.
24. In late 1991, the Financial section sent me to the Canary Islands to cover the aftermath of the suicide by press lord Robert Maxwell. I was asked to bulldog the autopsy report, while my colleague Paul Farhi was sent to London where most of the news about the collapse of Maxwell's empire was breaking. I was able to beat the competition (the NYT) on what the autopsy report would conclude. Paul Fahri, a reporter with less experience than me, received, I believe, merit increases in 1991 and in 1992 while I did not. In that year I also covered the collapse of Pan American World Airways and wrote an enterprise front page story: a tie test that tested the ability of consumers to differentiate the "silk" ties sold on the street versus designer ties and which reported on the actual fabric content, which was not silk.
25. In 1993, David Ignatius became Financial AME. A number of the women reporters in the section, very upset about treatment of women under AME Behr, asked to meet with Ignatius and his Deputy Doug Feaver to discuss issues that were of great concern to the women reporters. The event that triggered the meeting was the movement of reporter David Hilzenrath from the Real Estate Section to cover economics on Capitol Hill without the job having been posted or any of the current staff having been asked if they were interested. This was particularly troubling since high profile beats, such as economics, almost automatically went to men. Ignatius and Feaver met with about 6-8 women reporters. The subjects raised at that

26. In 1993, in part because of some serious family and personal problems and my need to be closer to home, I asked to cover local news. Financial AME David Ignatius approved my taking over the beat. I did a good job on the beat despite an extremely difficult personal situation which became increasingly complicated. I put together a focus group of area business leaders which resulted in an article that I wrote along with our polling director Rich Morin that elicited a note from Don Graham who referred to it as an "excellent article."
27. In 1994, I shifted to general assignments4 because I was concerned that my personal and family circumstances made it hard for me to cover a beat. I didn't want the newspaper to miss stories on a beat because I was called to a family emergency. But I also did important and high profile work for the Post during this period. For instance, the Financial section sent me to Latin America to cover the 1994 investment boom there. In my evaluation I was described as a "superior reporter" wit [sic] "a keen eye for offbeat but fascinating subjects" who is "skillful in explaining complicated stories."
28. In 1994, I received the Associated Press Mark Twain Award for coverage of the extreme cold snap that year. Although I was not an energy reporter at the time, I contributed to our coverage of how the utilities responded.

33. In 1998, with Ignatius' approval, I had planned to travel to Azerbaijan to write about oil and gas exploration by U.S. companies in the region and their efforts to build a pipeline to transport oil and gas from the region. I also had arranged with Mobil Oil, which was a local company notorious for being uncooperative with the press, to talk to their people in Azerbaijan about exploration and development activities there. Before I could make the trip, however, I was stopped. I was told that Dan Morgan (on the National staff) and David Ottaway (on Investigative) were working on a major take-out about oil and gas exploration in the Caspian region. I was the Post's oil and energy reporter, but that seemed to make no difference. They had staked out the territory, and I could not go. Glenn Kessler, who was my assignment editor, and David Ignatius, AME Financial, both told me they protested. However, I was blocked from taking the trip and doing the story in the manner I had planned. I later wrote a story about oil and gas exploration in the region from D.C. that led the Sunday Business section on 4/26/1998. This was another instance of my being big-footed to make way for male colleagues -- in this case on a story that was clearly on my beat.
34. Near the end of his tenure as AME, probably in 1997 or early 1998, AME David Ignatius told me that he had recommended me for a merit pay increase, but that it had been denied. He did not say why.
35. In 1999, I had 107 bylines, five of them on the front page. I had returned to general assignment reporting because of new family problems. I edited a Sunday section on utility deregulation at AME Jill Dutt's request, and I conceptualized, reported and wrote a Sunday Business section devoted to the subject of financial planning for retirement in September, 1999. Jill expressed her pleasure about the handling of both sections. "You are already doing a lot, leading the coverage on two ambitious Sunday packages of which I am very proud," she wrote in an e-mail October 12, 1999.

'36. In April 2000, I applied for and selected to cover the retailing and online commerce beat. It also drew praise from the head of Hechts, Frank Guzzetta, who said he had read everything written about this rival retailer and that my story was the best that had been done.
37. After September 11, 2001, I was called upon to help with the airline industry coverage because of my expertise in that area even though it was not part of my regular beat. I believe that I was being paid less than the person, i.e., Keith Alexander, who was the Post's regular airline industry reporter at the time. Keith was a relatively new Post employee at the time and had substantially less experience than did I. Between Sept. 11 and November 20, when I had surgery, I wrote six front page stories about the airline industry at the same time I continued to cover retailing. I am hoping it will be okay with Nancy if I use you more when I have these ledealls [sic: lede tails] where files from everyone need to be melded together." This was favorable commentary on my skill in writing a story composed of memos from several reporters in a way that made it seem seamless. Sept. 17, Sugawara e-mailed me: "I told Larry you are doing a fantastic job, and he wants to keep you in the thick of it."
38. I was one of a team of reporters awarded Second Place from the Maryland, Delaware, D.C. Press Association for continuing coverage of the financial aftermath of September 11, 2001.

'39. In October 2001, I was selected for a Knight Foundation for International Journalism fellowship to go to Mexico to work for six months with young journalists. At the request of AME Jill Dutt, who said she couldn't spare me, I asked the foundation to postpone the fellowship for up to two years.
40. On November 20, 2001 I had surgery. In the weeks leading up to the surgery, I suggested to Nancy McKeon and Jill Dutt that Dina ElBoghdady should begin taking over the retailing beat since I would be recuperating during the key holiday sales season.
41. In the week following surgery, I wrote part of a package for the Washington Post about the surgery in which I donated a kidney to Warren Brown, a colleague in the Financial News Section. It appeared December 11, 2001. This prompted a note from Don Graham which said "Thanks for (among many greater things) one of the best day's [sic] newspaper I've ever seen."
42. When I returned to work in late December 2001, I suggested to Jill Dutt that I continue as a general assignment reporter for a few months. Warren and I had been urged to write a book and were beginning to develop a proposal. I knew that, if we succeeded, I would need additional time off, so it didn't seem fair to start back on the retail beat only to have to drop it again.
43. From April 1, 2002 to August, 2002, I was on leave to write, with Warren Brown a book published by Public Affairs Press: Black & White and Red All Over - The Story of a Friendship. [underlined in original]
44. Upon returning from book leave, I asked to continue covering general assignments for the Business Section because I knew I would be away from work to promote the book. Earlier this month, for instance, Warren and I spent a week in Texas doing book-related appearances.
45. In August, 2002, AME Dutt asked me to edit a new half-page of editorial content to run in the Sunday papers. The page, called the JOBS page, began running on December 1, 2002. In situations where an employee works in more than one classification (e.g., non-supervisory editor position and a reporter position as I am presently doing), the
- 12 -'
p. 13 content 65597FED-3FDE-4F4D-B863-7CED6A9569EB_1_105_c.jpeg
collective bargaining agreement between the Post and the
Newspaper Guild provides that the person will receive
additional pay if s/he performs the second job for at least
one day a week. As a result, on the basis that I am
working in an editor position for two days per week, I am
currently being paid additional pay of $2,000 a year which
is more than the contractually required differential.
46. Throughout my career, I have been a prominent
advocate of better pay for women and minorities at the
Post. I did the first ever analysis for the Newspaper
Guild of pay by race and gender based on 1982 and 1986
salary information in a bulletin signed by me entitled "It
Doesn't pay to be Female or Black at The Washington Post."
(Attachment 1). This bulletin was widely circulated around
the Post. Cathy Hughes, the owner of Radio One, who had a
talk radio show that was popular with blacks, interviewed
Don Graham about the figures reported in the bulletin.
47. A few of my male colleagues, including Peter
Behr, took exception to my analysis. Peter told me that
what I viewed as discrimination was just the natural result
of most males having more seniority. I did a second
payroll analysis that grouped salaries by cohorts that had
been hired during the same five year period in an attempt
to answer this criticism. Even when adjusted (roughly) for
age and experience, the same patterns occurred in pay.
White males were the best paid, black females the worst.
The bulletin (signed by me) reporting on that rough
analysis is Attachment 2; this bulletin was widely
circulated around the paper.)
48. As mentioned above (para. 9), I also filed a
grievance over maternity leave in 1976 when I was not
allowed to take sick leave after my daughter was born. The
grievance was subsequently settled and the contract now
allows new mothers to use sick leave after the birth of
their babies. This was the result of my work on the
bargaining committee.
49. I played an active role in developing the EEO
charge which the Guild filed in 1988 (and which resulted in
the settlement agreement and arbitration procedure which
brings us here today). I interviewed individuals for
- 13 -
p. 14 content 05357027-99E8-4808-B6A6-BAA8AED82B0F_1_105_c.jpeg
[...top of paragraph 49 cut off:] the Guild and I helped collect signatures. I subsequently met with the EEOC, often during working hours. Post management was well aware of my activities.
50. I wrote Len Downie a memo in February 1986 about how two-tier pay had helped exacerbate what appeared to be discriminatory patterns in pay. We had a very pleasant lunch to discuss my memo, but he continued to disagree that there was discrimination.
51. I was one of several women who organized a meeting to discuss how women could protest management's failure in 1991 to act in the case of Juan Williams who had sexually harassed several women co-workers. Len Downie was aware of my role, because he asked me if he could address the group, which he did.
52. I was also one of a group of women who met with management to discuss the gossip an AME was spreading about a married female reporter, saying that she was sleeping with sources in the mid-1980s. I was one of six or seven women who met in 1996 to discuss the fact that there were so few women AMEs. And I was one of 8 to 10 women who met in 1993 with David Ignatius and Doug Feaver when they took over the Financial Section to discuss our complaints about biased treatment of women in the section under Peter Behr and our chagrin that he had filled an economics reporting beat with a man without letting others know that the beat was available.
I declare under penalty of perjury that the factual allegations contained in the foregoing Declaration are true and correct to the best of my personal knowledge and that I am competent to testify to such facts if called upon to do so.

Pay-discrimination statistical analyses and Guild salary printouts, 1982-1991

arbitration-exhibit not-asserted medium order medium read 1982-1991 11 pages

Subject: 'Pay Discrimination at the Washington Post: Preliminary Analysis and Findings' together with the Guild's supporting salary and merit-pay tabulations by race and sex.

Notes: Thematic grouping of the statistical case for pay discrimination. Order not asserted, though the material runs roughly from early Guild bulletins to the 1990 preliminary analysis and the 1991 merit-pay printouts.

p. 1 content 03AF6102-4345-4CEA-A036-1EE210277010_1_105_c.jpeg
- 2 -
[...Richman,] in response to a question, said white males would not be hurt by efforts to address this problem. The law is clear that discrimination cannot be remedied by taking something away from a non-discriminated group, he explained.
THE CONTRACT
Unit Chair Jill Nelson and Polaski said The Post's second walk-out from negotiations puts the contract issue back with the National Labor Relations Board, which the union expects will shortly issue an unfair labor practice complaint against the company.
At the recent bargaining meetings with The Post, the union presented revised contract proposals, then heard the company hold to its final offer and demand to see a final, bottom line offer from the Guild.
"My sense is that we went in emphasizing our flexibility in good faith," said Nelson. "In fact, it was as if we were being punished for being willing to negotiate."
Polaski said The Post's position did not appear to have any basis in law but may have been taken to underline management's insistence that the two parties are at impasse--a stance the Guild rejects.
"It was a big waste of our time," Polaski said.
She called the NLRB route "a slow, time-consuming process" but one that is necessary because the company's proposed contract--with its pay inequities, take-away medical plan and threats to union security and dues check-off--"continues to be unacceptable."
Outlining the history of contract negotiations since 1976, Polaski said The Post has achieved major take-aways in the past by wearing down the bargaining unit through protracted negotiations and concerted assaults on its membership jurisdiction.
If the unit doesn't resist this time, Polaski said, "the alternative is to sign another contract where we take a major step back--perhaps the last one" if the Guild loses union security or dues checkoff, as the company is proposing.
Members unanimously approved a motion from the floor directing the bargaining committee to continue its efforts to win a fair and just contract and expressing the unit's "anger and frustration" at The Post for its refusal to negotiate. The motion also expressed apprecia-tion to the bargaining committee for its hard work under very trying circumstances.
STOCKHOLDERS MEETING
Remember the "GREED" signs? Well, there's a chance to break them out again next Thursday, May 12, when The Post again hosts its annual stockholders meeting. The union invites members to greet the stockholders in front of the building that morning, beginning at 8:15 a.m. And Lew Diuguid (ext. 7410) is looking for employee stockholders to attend the meeting with him and ask a few pointed questions about the company's labor relations and discriminatory pay practices.
ORGANIZING
The Local is about to launch a new organizing campaign to sign up members, and it's going to need your help. You'll be hearing in the next couple of weeks about what you can do to help sign up members. (Note: new members will receive a complimentary ticket to the Celebrity Auction. For details on the auction, read on.)
CELEBRITY AUCTION: At last, some fun. Plans for the auction, "Bylines for Dollars", are being finalized. Renee Pouissant will serve as celebrity auctioneer and Bob Levey will emcee the June 8 festivities. It's at the Touchdown Club, 2000 L St., NW, and tickets ($20) will be available soon.
The live auction roster--Shales, Conconi, Gilliam, Millroy, Taylor, Rasberry, Kornheiser, Feinstein, Richman, Sherwood, Brooks, Perkins, Levey and 20 others--is filling up, but there's still room for a few more live "prizes".
And we're still collecting items for the silent auction (autographed copies of books by Post authors, home-cooked meals, political memorabilia, white elephants, dog sitting commitments, use of weekend get-away properties, etc.). AND, we're going to need some volunteers to help publicize the event, decorate the club, take tickets, oversee bidding, etc.
Contact Jim Yenckel (ext. 6563), Jill Nelson (ext. 7585), Linda Wheeler (ext. 7334) or Karlyn Barker (ext. 7296) if you've got skills, items or energy to spare.
We'll be putting out a complete program list soon--for Post employees and the general public.
- Karlyn Barker

[handwritten, top right: 1988 INFO]
The following charts show the widening dimensions of wage discrimination at The Post during the last five years. These figures cover employees in all professional, clerical and service jobs represented by the Guild.
THE PAY GAP WIDENS
WEEKLY PAY GAP BETWEEN
White Employees/Black Employees: 1982 $155 — 1987 $192 (scale $100–$200)
White Male Employees/White Female Employees: 1982 $165 — 1987 $204 (scale $100–$300)
White Employees/Hispanic Employees: 1982 $116 — 1987 $184 (scale $100–$200)
White Male Employees/Black Male Employees: 1982 $205 — 1987 $230 (scale $100–$300)
White Employees/Asian Employees: 1982 $135 — 1987 $163 (scale $100–$200)
White Male Employees/Black Female Employees: 1982 $244 — 1987 $332 (scale $100–$400)
These numbers are very disturbing. The situation need not exist. There can be no justification for wage discrimination, and The Washington Post does not even claim that it cannot afford to bring wages for women and minority employees up to those paid white men in the same jobs, with similar experience. There is an embarrassment of riches at The Post. The company made $132 million in profits in 1987, a jump of 152% over profits five years earlier. During those same five years, pay discrimination by The Post got worse.
The numbers speak for themselves.
The Washington-Baltimore Newspaper Guild
1511 K Street, NW #926
Washington, DC 20005
Contact: Sandi Polaski
(202)393-0808
p. 3 content 2AED39B4-071F-4C29-B36B-6B160B55A5C8_1_105_c.jpeg
—Post Unit News—
[logo: Newspaper Guild / AFL-CIO]
Washington/Baltimore Newspaper Guild
No. 15 Date May 6, 1988
GUILD MEMBERS MEET, VOTE ACTIONS
Guild members voted Wednesday to "hang tough" in the nearly two year effort to get The Post to bargain in good faith and address employees' concerns through negotiations.
RACE, SEX, AGE DISCRIMINATION
The unit also gave the union the go-ahead to file a race, sex and age discrimination charge against the company, which has stonewalled attempts at the bargaining table to correct blatant pay inequities among employees.
"It's a dramatic problem, it's a growing problem and it's a problem we can't run away and hide from," said Sandi Polaski, the Local's administrative officer.
So far, more than 100 employees have filled out questionnaires related to individual or general instances of sex, race or age discrimination. [Because the questionnaires were confidential, the content of the response is not discussed.] Those who have experienced discrimination but haven't submitted questionnaires can still do so, and can obtain them from Martha Hamilton, ext. 6700. [Please note that questionnaires should be returned directly to the attorneys whose names and address are listed on the questionnaire.]
The union, said Polaski, will decide shortly whether to file the charge with the Equal Employment Opportunities Commission or the D.C. Office of Human Rights. Both the EEOC and the human rights office have the power to subpoena data that is necessary for the discrimination case, and which The Post, until now, has refused to release to the union.
Polaski called The Post's pay policies "an irrational and unfair system" that is "purposely kept secret because it will not bear the light of day."
[Polaski cited the current issue of The Washingtonian-- which lists a number of salaries in the newsroom by name. Those salary figures, for those who haven't heard, were obtained by the magazine from court documents The Post supplied in connection with the federal overtime suit filed by a group of its employees. They got into print because The Post--which has refused to provide detailed salary data by name to the union for bargaining purposes-- failed to ask the court to keep those records confidential from the general public.]
Polaski said the pay gaps have increasingly widened over the years. Some examples: In 1982, the gap between white male reporters and white female reporters was $86 a week. By 1987, it had grown to $132 a week. The gap between all Latino employees in the Guild unit and all white employees grew from $116 a week in 1982 to $184 a week in 1987. The worst disparity was--and is--between white males and black females. In the reporter classification, that pay gap grew from $122 a week in 1982 to a shocking $240 a week in 1987. For all Guild jurisdiction employees, the gap between white males and black females hit an astonishing $332 a week in 1987.
Even when age and tenure at The Post are taken into account, dramatic disparities remain for women and minority employees.
Winn Newnam, an attorney consulted by the Guild because of his pre-eminence in handling discrimination cases, attended the meeting and said the data on hand indicates a clear pattern of pay discrimination.
Polaski said the local has petitioned the Guild international for funds to pursue the charge and expects to receive the money as a loan with no repayment terms--and the possibility it would be converted to a grant at a future date.
She also spoke to concerns that filing the discrimination complaint would harden the company's position at the bargaining table.
"There has not been a glimmer of movement" on the company's part, she said. "I don't see how their refusal to bargain could get any harder." "On this issue," she added, "The Post has not put one word on the table, has not put one cent on the table to address pay inequities."
- over -
Affiliated with American Federation of Labor-Congress of Industrial Organizations, Canadian Labour Congress.

[vertical bar chart; center line labeled 'AVERAGE REPORTER SALARY'; bars extending upward shown as positive deviation, downward as negative]
White Male+$69.15
Black Male-$13.98
White Female-$62.81
Black Female-$171.44
[vertical axis tick labels as printed: $100 / $50 above the average line; $50 / $100 / $150 / $200 below it]
Black women reporters at The Washington Post are paid $240 a week less, on average, than reporters who are white men. Even when salaries are adjusted to reflect the impact of years at the paper and age (a good indicator of previous experience), there is still a $170 a week disparity for black women reporters. White women reporters earn average paychecks that are $120 a week less than those of their white male colleagues of similar age and tenure at The Post.
DEVIATION
FROM AVERAGE REPORTER SALARY
BY RACE AND SEX
And the gaps are not just among reporters. For all employees represented by the Guild at The Post the average salary for white males is $204 a week ahead of the average salary for white females, $230 ahead of the average salary for black males, and a shocking $332 a week ahead of the average salary for black females.
Nor is the situation improving with time. It is getting worse. For black women reporters, the pay gap has actually doubled in five years.

September 14, 1993
Summary of statistical analysis for Newspaper Guild
Enclosed please find the lists, salary breakdowns and merit pay summaries, reflecting our best efforts at interpreting the Guild's request. The following decisions were made:
I. For the lists, a summary line was printed for each employee, rather than following the multi-line format of the original printout.
The list in order of salaries uses the first dollar amount on the first record for each employee.
The variables which summarize merit pay reflect only salary history records coded "14".
The "ID" value on each record corresponds to the order of that employee's salary history records on the original printout.
II. The salary breakdowns also use the first dollar amount on the first record for each employee.
III. Again, the merit pay averages by race, sex and year are based only on those salary history records coded "14".
Please note that only single year breakdowns were calculated, because only three years of salary histories were provided.
Merit pay averages include zero values, as they appeared in the data.
