Newsroom survey: volunteered employee comments on race and sex

report internal-pagination (2 of 14 pages) mixed order medium read 1986-1997 14 pages

Subject: Compiled staff-survey responses on race and gender relations, hiring, promotions and advancement at The Washington Post.

Notes: One survey compilation. The cover (January 1986) and the numbered introduction are placed first on the document's own pagination; the remaining twelve single-page response sheets (ten complete, two with torn edges) carry no reliable internal numbering, so their sequence is ours and is not asserted as Ashley's.

p. 1 cover C21A73F5-9BF6-4379-8395-90721733398D_1_105_c.jpeg
NEWSROOM SURVEY:
VOLUNTEERED COMMENTS
JANUARY 1986
[bleed-through of reverse-side text visible but not legible]
p. 2 content 3544C530-DAEC-4133-A139-938CED09C3CE_1_105_c.jpeg
[intro paragraph] Here are the comments handed in along with the survey questionnaires. There are more than 100 of them; the fact that they come from such a large percentage of the staff (unlike a small focus group session), done in private, gives us a very good complement to the survey. please note that the designation "white" really means non-black. — Sussman.
[left column]
White female, Metro, Reporter:
The Post is run by white males, that's clear by the masthead and glass offices lining the newsroom. Still, newspaper ought to be hired for just one reason — because they're good. Occasionally I've run into a staff member here who seemed to perhaps have been hired before they were seasoned enough for The Post and in the main they were black or Harvard graduates. There is a favoritism of connections here. A bigger problem than promoting blacks it seems to me is The Post's need to move women into higher news positions. Elsie Carter is not an example, Karen DeYoung is. The newsroom is jammed with female reporters and lower level editors but there ought to be more women, higher up.
That said, it's a great newspaper, I love being here and I think the black reporters, photographers and editors I've worked with have been extremely talented. I deeply regret the loss of Alice Bonner and think Post management could've and should've offered her the opportunities she sought and deserved — both as a black and a woman — to move up in management.
Black male, Reporter.
Re: Question 19 [how often denied particular assignment because of race]. If I were denied a particular assignment, I could only speculate if it were race, youth, inexperience, or a combination of factors. I have been denied assignments that I believe I am qualified for, and it could be because of race, although I cannot prove that.
Re: question 3-K [The Post wants to promote only a token number of blacks] I disagree that The Post only "wants" to promote a token number of blacks. I feel that from looking around that only a token number have been promoted. Saying that The Post "wants" to do this implies a deliberate, calculated policy, & I would think it is a more subconscious problem in not looking at blacks when considering promotions.
White female, Style/News Desk/Sports/Editorial/Other, Reporter:
Too little consideration is given to reporters for their desired career development here. No one ever asks, "Where do you want to be in five years?" or "Let's come up with a plan so that your needs and mine are met." A lot of good, talented people find it difficult to advance because they are not good verbal communicators and political operators.
White female, National/Financial/Investigative/Foreign, Editor:
I think The Post's main problem has been a failure to recruit qualified blacks, and of those recruited, a failure to carefully monitor, train, work with those who need extra help.
[right column]
Black male, Reporter
Many of the questions are phrased in ways that imply the answer.
I suspect The Post of being an integration-conscious poser in this survey.
Why doesn't The Post just initiate some measures instead of asking everybody?
A Black AME would be counterproductive — creating possible resentment among Hispanics, women, whites and other races. It certainly wouldn't be a solution to anything.
White female, working at The Post five years or less, older than 30, position not known:
I think that The Post's concern about hiring and keeping more minorities is admirable but more important is hiring qualified minorities. I know some people that have been hired based solely on race and they are not of equal ability with their peers. This hurts The Post and I feel it's detrimental to the minority races
Inequality within my department, like in the very low percentage of women. Less than 25 percent are women and no women occupy managerial positions.
White male, National/Financial/Investigative/Foreign, Editor
The Post has been irresponsible in seeking and keeping black reporters, in promoting black editors. It provides little or no support system for minorities.
White male, Style/News Desk/Sports/Editorial/Other, Editor:
My feeling is that the major problem that blacks and women face is not getting hired but in getting promoted, especially to the top ranks. Just take a look at the AMEs and higher: two women (one in charge of "administration," the other over the Style news, former women's section), one black (again in a post where news judgement is not a major factor).
That's why I found Q1 unanswerable. I also think The Post could and should be more aggressive than it is in recruiting blacks and in training them. Training around here has been inadequate the whole time I've worked here.
I have no problems in signing my name to these comments or my answers as a whole.
B.McKayE
White male, National/Financial/Investigative/Foreign, Editor:
The Post's main weakness in minority hiring is its automatic gravitation to the same affluent, well-bred Ivy League types courted by every other paper. It should concentrate on recruiting people straight out of school, purposely avoiding the Ivy League.
[page number at bottom: 1]
p. 3 content C4FAD717-8AD6-40C8-9526-FD6D7D5E5FBE_1_105_c.jpeg
order not asserted here: not-asserted
[Left column] We don't need quotas. We need quality. And that should apply to white men, white women, black men, black women, Hispanics, staff members and so forth. (I think the survey is flawed in omitting questions about hiring and treatment of gays, given the large number on the news staff.)
We have too much dead wood around here and some of it stems from hiring a woman just to have a woman in a particular position. The same goes for blacks.
I encourage The Post to recruit as many minorities as possible and establish a specific training program for them. But this problem exists because it has been ignored for years. Likewise, it's not going to be corrected in a few months or even in a few years. It may take literally years (until some of our younger AMEs are ready to retire) before we have a quality, well-balanced newsroom that is color-blind and oblivious to sexual preference. I just hope we don't try to solve the problem overnight. The problem is rooted in bias and that is erased only through generations working hard to overcome that bias.
Black male, Reporter.
This survey stinks.
How out of touch can you be?
Why are you asking me these questions? Your perceptions count, not mine.
It would be so unnecessary, were The Post true to itself. I feel perfectly certain that the results will be ignored, in any case. To the powers, this questionnaire itself is very likely the answer.
I'm black. A good reporter and writer. I've done excellent and exciting work here . . . Yet I'm professionally unhappy. I perceive more and more career doors closed to me and feel race has something to do with it.
When I was hired . . . I thought the sky was the limit for my career. But recently I've been feeling left out. I can't say I see much [sic: 'muck'] here anymore.
I was really naive. The newsroom has become little more than a sad reflection of American society, white guys running a show according to rules of their own devising.
I was able to play a role for a long time. But you get real tired of being a corporate negro, especially if you recognize the inherent limits. I do have a chip on my shoulder. And I don't want to ignore it anymore.
I wish I [could] understand the rules you guys use to play your game. How is it that some folks are anointed and their careers are planned years in advance? When I hear edicts drop from on high that So-and-So is going to Tokyo in 1987, or So-and-So is moving to national, or they WANT So-and-So to go to Texas or Mexico City, how is it that such things are decided?
Is it as simple as ambling into Len's office and saying, 'I want'? Is it as simple as merit? Of course not. In any organization there's favoritism. And in this organization, like most, it's the favoritism of the white male.
But what's really depressing is the paper itself. It just doesn't have a heart anymore. I don't know where it went. Metro should be its heart, but all anyone cares about is covering his ass.
When was the last time we really had a passion about something? Passion and commitment cut across all racial and ethnic bounds. That's what really turned me on about The Post original. And what saddens me most. I don't think many people here care much anymore.
I'd love to be metro editor for a week. I'd still be writing the hell out of that PCP story and have six people on it full-time. Maybe I should talk to the AME/Minority about that. Style is a joke, too. I'm a damn good writer. When was the last time Mary Hadar wanted scuttlebutt [? 'scuttle the newsroom'?—reads 'wanted scuttlewt the newsroom'] for a writer, much less a black writer? Who IS Mary Hadar?
[Right column, top continues:] or it's too mysterious. And the magazine. How many blacks have been hired for our new $25 million magazine?
But you know, it's really not a matter of numbers. You can pidgeonhole [sic] people until the cows come home and it won't have any bearing at all on whether your denizens are happy.
The more you try to categorize — with great ideas such as this survey — the more offensive you are.
A special AME to provide "advice and assistance for blacks." Christ. That's enough to make me cry. When that day comes it truly won't be worth staying here anymore.
White female, National/Financial/Investigative/Foreign, Reporter.
Management tends to have a confrontational attitude toward all employees that far more demoralizing than mere racial or sex discrimination. Why are we the enemy when we're all trying to get the same job done?
A black-specialist AME would ghettoize the people concerned and relieve other AMEs of needing to worry about the issue. What's next, a Black News' page?
How can we judge our pay positions when pay data is kept from us by management?
White male, National/Financial/Investigative/Foreign, Reporter.
A concerted attempt to hire blacks and women is not an igno[si]ble activity — there is little doubt of past prejudice and, to a lesser extent, current prejudice in society.
But hiring anyone — black, white, Oriental, male or female — for reasons other than qualifications demeans everyone here. That's why many of the questions in the survey cannot be answered adequately.
I think this newspaper is often a victim — hiring promising young black reporters who then get hired away. But even tho[ugh] all things equal — a black probably has better advancement opportunities. I think personnel policies appear sufficiently confusing to all races and sexes that a bright black reporter or editor, like a white reporter or editor, may not know what, if anything, The Post has in store until the decision to leave is made.
It's the often chaotic, hard-to-fathom hiring and promotion decisions that employees of all races sometimes find hard to take.
White male, National/Financial/Investigative/Foreign, Reporter.
The biggest problems have probably resulted from personal favoritism — white or black — with a tendency to judge people frivolously and to get overenthusiastic or overcritical about someone based on personal qualities, not professional. I think the si[t]uation has improved substantially in the past few years — i[t]'s clear why — but measurably.
White male, National/Financial Investigative/Foreign, Editor.
Despite the arguments about quotas, The Post should go out of its way to hire and promote the best people for the jobs, regardless of race. Countless times, hiring a minority or woman just to meet a quota has resulted in hiring someone without the skills to do the job, putting an extra burden on co-workers and creati[ng] resentment that a qualified person — white, black, male or fe[m]ale — was not hired. My department has been understaffed[?] for months, forcing many of us to work unpaid overtime, be[ca]use management can't find a "qualified" minority hire to fill it. Me[an]while, we have competent part-timers aplenty who can't get hire[d] because of petty reasons.
2
p. 4 content DFEF0E89-81BD-4994-B2E4-06B40FB9775A_1_105_c.jpeg
order not asserted here: not-asserted
[left column, header partly cut off at top: '...e male, National/Financial/Investigative/Foreign, Reporter:']

[left column]
White male, Reporter:
I can only speak about my experience and impressions in the financial section, which suggest a couple of conclusions.
I don't believe there are major barriers to advancement for women, blacks and other minorities in our section. I think there are some cultural gaps between the white males who run the section and the women reporters who work in it. Our section is still a white male operation and understandably there is irritation about that among some of the women on the staff. But I don't believe any doors are closed to them. I think reporters in the section — blacks, women and men — are essentially assessed on a color-blind, gender-blind basis.
Nevertheless, I don't think The Post will or should be satisfied with its performance in this area unless some additional measures are taken.
We need to work even harder at identifying women, blacks and other minorities with potential to be high-impact reporters or managers. These people should be on the fast track. And then, there needs to be a program to help equip them to succeed.
We have provided educational opportunities, and we need to do even more. For women and minorities who want to move into newsroom management, I'm sure there are useful training opportunities outside the building that could be taken advantage of. I have misgivings about setting up someone outside the chain of command in the newsroom to review minority issues. But if we do establish a kind of opportunity ombudsman, this person's office should be open to the white journalists too. In other words, if someone is appointed to follow up on the survey issues, it needs to be a comprehensive contact with the entire staff, as the survey was.
Beyond that, we could be more creative about moving people with potential around the newsroom. Why not take a younger black reporter who has shown a lot of potential and assign that person to financial for a couple of years, for example, with the goal of broadening that reporter's experience? That reporter could then return as an assignment editor in financial later on and move into the editing ranks that way.
Why pick financial? Because it's an expanding section — we know there will be more room here than elsewhere on the fifth floor. And if we want to break new ground, we shouldn't get hung up too much on slot issues and requiring trade-offs for such assignments. We should just do it.
We don't have enough information about the very best women, blacks and other minority candidates outside the paper. We have picked up some great new talent for the financial section in recent years, but I'm not aware that we had a really strong selection list of minority reporters from which to choose.
How to cure that? I'd make some time in the schedules of the deputy AMEs to meet regularly with Wilkinson and Signer to develop a stronger scouting report, with some specific goals about screening, evaluating and recruiting people we want.
Finally, my two-cents worth: I think that women, blacks and other minorities in our society constitute a great, vital and still-un[der]tapped reservoir of talent for newspapers.
We had better tap that reservoir, because we need the talent, and because the unique perspectives they bring about their own parts of society will be an increasingly important source of news for all our readers.
In that context, I think white reporters and editors at The Post would understand why special efforts were being made to tap that source of talent, for the benefit of the entire enterprise.
This survey is a good idea.
[right column]
No I.D. at All:
It is worth noting that the survey ignores a significant minority population of The Post — gays. The existence of this minority has never been acknowledged or embraced by management as other groups have been.
Clearly, this is a separate issue and probably should not be dealt with in this survey. The nature and extent of discrimination are different. And there is no government pressure to avoid discrimination against gays.
However, the taking of this survey seems to be a good time to remind people in authority that there is potential for discrimination against people because of their sexual preference, that there are no doubt people who could cite instances of it, and that it is likely to become worse in light of the AIDS epidemic.
No I.D. at All:
Some of the survey questions ask for racial stereotyping, which is repugnant.
The Post should seek out and advance the best reporters and editors it can find whatever their race or sex.
It is reasonable to make special efforts to recruit blacks, especially people who are familiar with the areas we cover. Also, managers should take into account particular strengths and interests in making particular assignments. But there should be no "double standards," goals, or quotas based on race.
Post management should treat all employees fairly and without favoritism or discrimination. A policy of ethic [sic] preference is divisive and demeaning — wrong for whites, wrong for WASPs, wrong for blacks, wrong in South Africa, wrong here.
Even when well-intentioned, a preference or "set aside" for blacks in a white controlled organization, such as The Post, is based ultimately on an offensive and mistaken assumption of black inferiority. Perversely, it has the effect of fostering inequality and prejudice, not overcoming them.
I deeply want a society in which people are treated as individuals, offered equal opportunities and judged by equal standards. The Post should operate that way itself.
There should be more assistance for new reporters — white or black — whom management wants to take a gamble on and develop. But there should be no separate "track" for blacks at The Post and no "Black AME."
No I.D. at All:
No articles by women in The Post magazine special issue except the fashion articles!

[LEFT COLUMN] White female, Style/News Desk/Sports/Editorial, Editor:
It seems to me that blacks and whites at The Post share some common complaints. The lack of opportunity to move around here, to change jobs, is very frustrating. There are many people who are not interested in management positions but would just like the chance to move to a better job or even move "sideways" to a new department to use their talents in different ways and avoid burnout on the job they are doing.
People feel that you have to be "in," you have to be on the "star track" or someone's protege or pal to get anywhere here. Jobs are filled before they are ever posted and since personnel moves are made behind the scenes, most people are completely shut out of the whole process. It's a continual sore point, of course, that so many jobs are filled with people brought in from the outside; they always seem to be more "qualified." It's sort of a Catch-22: You work for one of the world's greatest newspapers and you can't get on-the-job training. If you can't learn what you need to know about journalism at The Washington Post, where can you?
In a recent memo signed by Tom Ferguson which was posted on the bulletin board, he announced some management moves and said they illustrated The Post's commitment to "promotion from within, mobility between departments, and development of people." This commitment has been mighty inconsistent and comes as news to a lot of people.
Symptomatic of this problem are the comments heard about this survey — largely to the effect of, what's the point? Nothing is going to change. It's just The Post's way of patting itself on the back for pretending to do something. Here's hoping that doesn't turn out to be the case.
White female, Editor:
At issue is not so much how The Post trains, keeps and promotes minority reporters and editors. Rather, the issue is how does The Post manage its people — all its people. I have worked at previous papers for a variety of editors with a range of management styles and I imagine myself to be a good manager of people. Accordingly, I am continually struck by how poorly The Post does in all areas of management — from hiring to training to career development.
The Post knows how to reward and how to punish; it does not know how to work with an employee to find solutions to problems that will best serve the employee as well as the paper. What is particularly disturbing to me is that I sometimes wonder if The Post truly is committed to improving its management of people. I say that because I routinely see flagrant violations of basic management tenets — i.e., reporters finding out about changes in their status through the office grapevine; people applying for positions and never given the courtesy of being told they weren't selected for the job and why; supervisors publicly badmouthing people who work for them. At The Post, it sometimes seems, such practices are not violations, but standard operating procedure.
I also would like to note my objection to this survey. It is widely perceived in the newsroom that this survey is the result of Alice Bonner's decision to resign The Post and accept a job at USA Today. A black woman leaves the paper and The Post wants to know why. That reaction suggests that the only way Alice was viewed by The Post was as a black woman instead of as a capable editor with a vast knowledge of the city and a good sense of people. Perhaps this is such thinking — which reduces people to categories — that prompted Bonner's departure and that leads me to reach my opinion about The Post management of people.

White male, Sports, Editor:
1) In summary, I would say The Post is better than most other newspapers I've been at with regard to working conditions and positions for blacks and women, but it could be much better.
2) I think the area The Post is weakest in is recruitment and training of all young reporters/editors but especially blacks and a lesser degree women. My observation, after just less than one year here, is that The Post uses a sink-or-swim approach to training reporters and interns rather than a training/nurturing approach. This is fine for those who can "swim" right away, but there does not seem to be a strong systematic mechanism for helping average young reporters/editors improve. I would be happy to share thoughts on how to improve this situation and I would be unhappy to discuss them with any management people at any time. — Signed, William O'Brien
White female, Metro, Editor:
On the subject of race, it seems to me the question is not quotas for hiring or promotions, but simply hiring the best possible people. It's tokenism to hire blacks in numbers simply because they're black. They should be allowed to compete, both with other blacks and with whites and the best one should simply be hired and allowed to advance without regard to their race.
The idea of an AME for personnel is a good one, but it should not be just to monitor the career progress of any one particular group. The problem with The Post is not racial or sex discrimination, but simply favoritism in general. AMEs have a "star" system that's reinforced by the fast-tracking of specific reporters and editors who are chosen not always because of their ability but rather because they are personal friends of are well liked because they don't threaten by having unpopular opinions.
The Post's greatest shortcoming as far as personnel matters is concerned is in assuming that certain reporters and editors want to do something. Often, career decisions are made based on what is believed to be best for the paper without any consideration for what's best for the individual involved or whether that person actually wants to follow that career path.
People often don't speak up about these situations because they feel that to do so will endanger their future possibilities for advancement or will be held against them, so they wind up doing things they don't want to do or don't believe are the best use of their abilities. There's really no unbiased person in the newsroom: that anyone can comfortably talk with about such situations.

Managers tend to pick one or two persons in their departments whom they nurture and promote, ignoring all the other talents available. [bottom edge cut off]

White male, Metro, Reporter:
I think The Post needs to be more aggressive and thorough in finding minority reporters. But more importantly, we need to do a better job of supervising and training minority and beginning reporters at all races after they are hired. Too often young reporters are allowed to sink or swim on their own without having editors who have the time to work with them individually on a regular basis. Assignment editors are often too busy getting the next day's paper out and do not have the time to work with young reporters and really develop them.
One suggestion would be to have regular training seminars with small groups of reporters in which senior reporters and editors can explain how to use public records, how to develop sources and other basic skills. In the rare cases in which this is done usually the speakers are reporters like Broder and Woodward who are well-known and interesting, but are not close to the kind of reporting problems beginning reporters encounter.
In addition, these training seminars should be held over a set period of time in which the same people work with each other and discuss every-day problems. One other area that needs more attention is that in addition to more black reporters we need a lot of work on other minorities, particularly Chicanos, Puerto Ricans and Asians.

Unidentified:
I think this survey and the resurgence of interest in hiring, training and promoting minorities is a good thing for the paper. And I'm glad managers worry about how blacks are treated here. But I've got to say that I don't think The Post has all that many problems with the way it treats minorities — it has a problem with the way it treats people.
I think The Post would be doing itself and all employees a favor by hiring better qualified employees in general. And if you hire better qualified blacks you won't have a problem with promotions. Right now, there are too many "marginal hires" that come back to haunt the news operation, especially when the paper periodically goes looking for minorities to promote.
A Milton Coleman, Gene Robinson or Keith Richburg is not going to have a problem at The Post. They wouldn't if they were white, either. They're just too good. But there have been too many hires over the years—blacks, women, whites—that shouldn't have been made. The result is an embarrassing chunk of the staff that isn't as good—and never will be as good—as it should be.
The marginal employees struggle here their entire careers or else settle into a comfortable niche that doesn't demand too much. They are assigned around, carried by better reporters, not used as much, not taxed too often and almost always re-written. This isn't good for the paper and it's not good for the employees, including the better reporters and editors who have to shoulder the burden of weaker ones. And with black employees, unfortunately, the problem can become more difficult since the paper really wants to do right by them.
[continues mid-sentence at top of right column:] But this is not a training paper — never has been and probably never will and never should be. Maybe the paper can afford to "train" on a small scale — and, if so, it should start with some of the people on board now. But what usually happens is that a marginal black is hired, either outright or as an intern, and then often gets carried rather trained. They fall into the categories mentioned above. If they're lucky they learn a lot on their own and may even progress, especially when the paper starts feeling bad about lack of promotions among blacks.
Wouldn't it be better to make better hiring choices in the beginning so that The Post can make intelligent promotions that are good for the employee and the paper — and not just promotions that look good.
What's the point, for instance, of hiring a Michel Marriott, a nice, sweet guy with a frequently nice touch to his writing if the paper wasn't prepared to help him learn how to be a reporter? He didn't know how to do interviews, he didn't know how to judge the material he gathered, he wasn't careful, and it frequently took him all night marathons at the VDT just to get his nice writing touches down.
Yet he was initially told how good he was, got a big rush here and an even bigger build-up when the Times (no better than The Post in these matters of minority hiring) seemed hot for him. And when opinion on him turned, with never any effort to train him at all, he cut a deal elsewhere. No one, least of all him, was helped in the process.
Unidentified:
What about older people? What is the population of the country, or this city, of people over 50? What percentage of hires has The Post made of people over 50? Young blacks and women may not be interested but one day they will reach 50.
Any worthwhile survey of personnel policy should include this group. The number of people over 50 who have been "pushed" in recent years is no secret, despite laws to the contrary. After decades of substantial and pivotal contributions, they wake up one day to learn they are not contributors and are "useless."
This has gone on long enough and devasted enough people for it to be included in any such survey. Maybe the survey should include those who have left with a bitterness that wiped out memories of otherwise joyful years on the paper.
White female, Style/News Desk/Sports/Editorial/Other, Editor:
I don't believe any kind of racial quota should be aimed for at The Post. I believe the most qualified applicants should be hired with no regard or preference to race.
White female, Style, Reporter:
I think most of these questions are impossible to answer with a simple number or phrase.
Within Style, I feel a constant strain of well-disguised sexism and racism shape story choices and reporter assignment. Women get assigned to social and frivolous stories more often than men. We do not cover enough elements of black Washington, a problem that obviously afflicts the entire paper.
White male, Photo/News Art:
Overall The Post is great place to work and run by wonderful people.

Black male, Reporter:
The problem at The Post is not so much racism, in the sense of managers not wanting to see minorities promoted, as an inability of the managers to see potential in people who are unlike themselves. There is, in addition, a tendency on the part of top management to put too low a ceiling on minorities, that is to think primarily of white men when it comes to grooming people for higher-level jobs.
There has been a welcome trend in recent years of placing black reporters in jobs and/or sections of the paper that they used not to be considered for — National/Financial/Investigations/Foreign. Financial, etc. But there seems to be the sense in the newsroom that (Matt Lewis notwithstanding) city editor is about the top job any black reporter/editor should aspire to.
The Post frequently seems to interpret equal opportunity as meaning that if minorities and women work hard and follow directions, they too can become white men. I'm thinking particularly of (can I use a name?) Eddie Sargent, a nondirectional hire who, because of background, could bring us stories and insights into a segment of the city that we've never covered adequately. That was a gutsy idea. But Eddie is now floundering because his editors seem to see him as an inadequate suburban white man. For example, he could be bringing us important insights into the problems inner city youngsters face in school; some sense of how young black men cope (or fail to cope) in a middle-class town, or what tempts them into criminality. But we have him covering schools in the most traditional sort of way, a fact that magnifies his weaknesses and hides his potential strengths.
Patrice Gaines-Carter is another wasted opportunity. Instead of looking for ways to capitalize on her insights, sensitives and fine writing, the desk seems to be trying to turn her into just another suburban white reporter. Somebody should be working with her to develop ideas and assignments that allow us to take advantage of her special gifts.
I'm not really crazy about a special AME for minority staffers — a Purite Victorious "deputy for the colored" — but it might make sense for Ben or Len to have regular meetings with a small group of black staffers in order to get them a sense of where the trouble spots and squandered opportunities might be.
White female, Metro, Reporter:
All you have to do is look at The North Wall for the answer to question 1. The Post simply doesn't pay enough attention to all people's careers and the result is that white males, who started off with the advantage in the early 70s, stay ahead.
It does not train editors and does not make sure that blacks get the reporting background they need before moving ahead. The result is damaged careers and blacks leaving. In other cases, they are shunted aside and their knowledge of the city ignored by managers who think being a white male is an important criterion for excellence.
[right column]
A corporate culture prevails that continues to favor white males in everything from salaries to assignments and promotions — and the paper continues to hire more males from outside for managerial positions instead of promoting women and blacks who are here. Thus, the managerial pool remains white male. Readers are robbed of a diversity of judgments as to what is news — and people who work here continue to lose opportunities to advance. If someone is appointed to watch over careers, he or she should be charged with monitoring and helping everyone's career — perhaps with special emphasis on correcting discriminatory practices that harm blacks and women.
I do think The Post has made tremendous progress in helping parents to combine work and family, which has helped a number of women continue their careers after having babies — and kept them in the management pool.
White male, section blank, Reporter:
The root of the problem is a failure to cultivate black journalists at the high school and college levels. The ranks of black journalists should be expanded, but not on a knee-jerk basis. Of course, the paper should also compete vigorously for qualified blacks in provincial areas, as well as make a greater effort to sponsor promising candidates from local universities such as Howard and UDC.

White male, Metro Reporter:
I think:
1) The Post has done an OK job in hiring and promoting blacks, though there's plenty of room for improvement.
2) I do not think there is racism at The Post, and I think some blacks use that charge as a crutch. What The Post is a brutal, tough, often unforgiving place to work. There's lots of fear about one's standing here — fear brought to life by the seemingly arbitrary destruction of careers of even some fine journalists among us. This fear and anxiety — generated as much by one's peers on the staff as by one's superiors — affects blacks and whites.
I think some blacks may be made slightly more tense and uncomfortable than whites in this atmosphere — but only slightly.
I believe The Post has a long way to go in making this place a more collegial place for all of us. Can we reduce the tension level and mean-spiritedness in this newsroom? Ask the editors of the Philadelphia Inquirer how they do it. Believe me, there's a stark difference in the atmosphere in the two newsrooms.
White female, Style/News Desk/Sports/Editorial/Other, Editor:
I have no doubt that when it comes to the notion of affirmative action, and the hiring and promotion of women and minorities, The Post's heart is in the right place. Unfortunately, I don't think its head always is.
Since my ultra-liberal college days, my views on affirmative action have been tempered somewhat by experiencing first-hand the negative effects of-promoting both blacks and women before they are truly ready to fill a post. (And I speak as a woman who quite likely might not have been hired if a desk hadn't lost their lone woman at the time.)
Since I've been at The Post, I've felt it does an inadequate job of identifying future managers and training them over time to assume positions of responsibility. Instead, it typically decided a good reporter can overnight become a good manager/editor, or that someone, by reaching a certain age has automatically gained the wisdom that goes along with it.
For instance, never in my time at The Post has anyone done anything as simple as organizing a brown-bag lunch of assignment editors to discuss issues like who gets certain assignments, working around family schedules, even current Post policy on handling certain kinds of stories. Often, these are grist for Pugshead discussions, but rarely does the word trickle down to the people who are on the line every day, making these kinds of decisions, working with reporters, evaluating people's work.
I think the problems that may have led some talented black staffers to leave in recent months are shared by many staffers, men and women, black and white. People do not have a sense of where they are going at The Post. They do not get enough feedback until it is too late, nor are they given the chance to talk with the people who count.
I personally feel fortunate that I have enough contact with upper management to make my own needs and desires known — and over time, they have been recognized. But I know many people for whom this is not the case, people who toil day after day, without anyone knowing how they really feel about what they are doing, or how they might be put to work more productively for the paper. This leads to frustration and deteriorating morale. The black reporters, I would suggest, are the ones to leave, simply because it is easier for them to find a home elsewhere.
In recent months, I have become concerned because it seems to me that The Post is developing quite a competent group of middle level female managers (i.e. assignment editors), many of whom at the top of The Post going to require a relentless devotion to say in 10 or 15 years, when their children are grown will these wo[men...]
[continues in right column]
[Right column]
men be able to climb back onto the ladder, without this time becoming victims of discrimination based on age (a bias, by the way, that I think may be the most prevalent at The Post). This is also a concern for many young male reporters.
There will always be tension at The Post because there is such a large staff and only a few real managerial positions. I am not about to argue that 40 percent of the AMEs should be women and 20 percent of them black — I couldn't begin to name candidates who are ready for those jobs. On the other hand, management continues to import white males for major managerial jobs to head the magazine, the Outlook section, the art department, even the deputy Metro editor. Is the ol' boy network alive and well? Can you honestly say that there is/was no woman or black in American journalism qualified for that job — as long as you're going to open up the search to outsiders?
I think this survey is a positive first step — but I think it is only that, a first step.

White male, Style/News Desk/Sports/Editorial, Editor:

White male, Metro, Reporter:
I favor more vigorous recruitment of blacks for news jobs at The Post, but oppose extraordinary efforts to promote once they're at work here. Promotion is zero-sum game: the advantage of one group is the disadvantage of another. The newsroom ought to be a free market of talent, with the cream (black or white) rising to the top.
If management wants to see more blacks in supervisory roles, let it achieve that goal by hiring more top-notch blacks in the first place — NOT by tilting things to their favor after they've arrived. The latter policy can only further polarize an already somewhat racially divided newsroom. The former harms no one; except hopeful job seekers, to whom The Post has no responsibility.

White female, Style/News Desk/Sports/Editorial, Editor:
I have no doubt that when it comes to the notion of affirmative action, and the hiring and promotion of women and minorities. The Post's heart is in the right place. Unfortunately, I don't think its head always is.
Since my ultra-liberal college days, my views on affirmative action have been tempered somewhat by experiencing first-hand the negative effects of promoting both blacks and women before they are truly ready to fill a post. (And I speak as a woman who quite likely might not have been hired if a desk hadn't lost their lone woman at that time.)
Since I've been at The Post, I've felt it does an inadequate job of identifying future managers and training them onto time to assume positions of responsibility. Instead, it typically decides a good reporter can overnight become a good manager/editor, or that someone, by reaching a certain age as automatically gained the wisdom that goes along with it.
For instance, never my in my time at The Post has anyone done anything as simple as organizing a brown-bag lunch of assignment editors to discuss issues like who gets current assignments, working around family schedules, even current Post policy on handling certain kinds of stories. Often, these are grist for Fogwash discussions, but rarely does the word trickle down to the people who are on the line every day, making these kinds of decisions, working with reporters, evaluating people's work.
I think the problems that may have led some talented black staffers to leave in recent months are shared by many staffers, men and women, black and white. People do not have a sense of where they are going at The Post. They do not get enough feedback until it is too late, nor are they given the chance to talk about how they feel about their job and where they want to go — with the people who count.
I personally feel fortunate that I have had enough contact with upper management to make my own needs and desires known — and over time, they have been recognized. But I know many people for whom this is not the case, people who toil day after day without anyone knowing how they really feel about what they are doing, or how they might be put to work more productively for the paper. This leads to frustration and deteriorating morale. The black reporters, I would suggest, are the ones to leave, simply because it is easier for them to find a home elsewhere.
In recent months, I have become concerned because it seems to me that The Post is developing quite a competent group of mid-level female managers (i.e. assignment editors), many of whom are in their mid-30s and have small children. Is a career path to the top of The Post going to continue to require a relentless devotion to the paper day on through every phase of one's career? Or, say, in 10 or 15 years, when their children are grown will these women be able to climb back onto the ladder, without this time becoming victims of discrimination based on age (a bias, by the way, that I think may be most prevalent at The Post). This is also a concern for many young male reporters.
There will always be tension at The Post because there is such a large staff and only a few real managerial positions. I am not about to argue that 40 percent of the AMEs should be women and 20 percent of them black. I can't begin to name candidates who are ready for those jobs. On the other hand, management continues to import white males for major managerial jobs — to head the magazine, the Outlook section, the art department, etc. Can you honestly say that there is no woman or black in American journalism qualified for that job — as long as you're going to open up the search for outsiders?
I think this survey is a positive first step — but I think it is only that — a first step.
DOC-008

Martha 11

Agreed Arbitration Procedures for Compensation Discrimination Claims

arbitration-exhibit explicit high order medium read 1994 onward 6 pages

Subject: The Washington Post / Washington-Baltimore Newspaper Guild agreed procedures governing compensation-discrimination claims filed under the negotiated settlement.

Notes: Complete six-page agreement. Page order is explicit: Ashley named the files 'Arbitation Procedures pg 1/3/4/6' and kept the existing scans of pages 2 and 5. Her four re-scans replace the earlier low-resolution copies of pages 1, 3, 4 and 6, which are recorded as superseded.

p. 1 content martha11-pg1.jpeg
Ashley's file: Arbitation Procedures pg 1.jpeg · replaces B559737D-ADA7-42C7-922A-A0CF2AE0C024_1_105_c.jpeg
[Superseded scan was photographed sideways; this image is upright.] ARBITRATION PROCEDURES
These procedures are established for the resolution of certain individual claims of discrimination on the basis of gender, race (i.e., Black, White, Asian, Native American), and/or Hispanic origin in wages or other compensation in Guild-covered positions. Claims may be asserted by current employees who occupy Guild-covered positions or who have occupied Guild-covered positions at any time between September 1, 1994 and the date of the Negotiated Settlement Agreement. Claims shall be limited to claims of discrimination in compensation on the basis of gender, race, and/or Hispanic origin after August 31, 1994. Such claims shall not be barred on the ground that the initial act of discrimination occurred before September 1, 1994. In all cases, however, relief shall be limited to the period beginning September 1, 1994. Claims shall be limited to compensation claims and shall not include claims based on allegedly discriminatory promotions, transfers, assignments or other matters that might affect compensation. Filing a claim under these procedures will waive any right the employee otherwise might have to file the claim in any agency or court.
Claims will be heard by an arbitrator agreed upon by the Guild and the Post, and shall be subject to the following procedures:
1. Claims must be made in writing on a claim form that is available from the Personnel Department or the Guild. Claim forms must be delivered, or postmarked for delivery, to Carol McNamee, Vice President Government Affairs, The Washington Post, [text continues on next page, not photographed]
p. 2 content 04894C59-F94F-419D-9BEE-B15C1D72482D_1_105_c.jpeg
Ashley's file: Arbitation Procedures pg 2.jpeg
[rotated 90 degrees]
1150 15th Street, N.W., Washington, D.C. 20071 within sixty (60) days of the date of the mailing of the Joint Statement announcing the Negotiated Settlement Agreement. Any claim not filed within that time is deemed to be waived. The Post will provide the Guild with copies of claim forms within seven (7) days of their receipt.
2. a. Immediately following the execution of this agreement, the Post and the Guild shall meet and attempt to identify at least four mutually acceptable arbitrators with knowledge of and experience with the handling of Title VII discrimination claims. Final agreement upon arbitrators to be used shall occur within thirty (30) days after expiration of the time for filing claims, at which point, taking into account the number of claims that are filed, the Guild and the Post shall agree on a single arbitrator to hear all claims or a list of arbitrators to be assigned to claims on an appropriate basis.
b.1. If within thirty (30) days after the expiration of the time for filing claims the Guild and the Post cannot agree on arbitrators, or on another method for selecting arbitrators, three arbitrators (or such other number as the Post and the Guild may agree upon) shall be chosen from a list provided by the American Arbitration Association ("AAA") (or, if AAA will not provide such a list, JAMS-Endispute or other organization to be agreed upon) of arbitrators with knowledge of and experience with the handling of Title VII discrimination claims. Each party shall strike names on an alternating basis
- 2 -
p. 3 content martha11-pg3.jpeg
Ashley's file: Arbitation Procedures pg 3.jpeg · replaces D33544E8-5838-4476-B66D-07BBA3CF7BA7_1_105_c.jpeg
[Superseded scan was photographed sideways; this image is upright.]
from the AAA list until three (or another agreed-upon number) remain.
b.2. If the Guild and the Post cannot agree on a method for assigning individual claims, they will be assigned in alphabetical order based on the last names of the claimants and the arbitrators.
3. The Post shall serve an initial response upon the claimant or the claimant's designated representative within 20 days of the assignment of an arbitrator to hear the claim.
4. In addition to the initial claim and response, both parties may submit affidavits, documents and other information to the arbitrator. The arbitrator may communicate directly with the parties, jointly or separately, and may request documents and information deemed relevant to the claim. All material submitted to the arbitrator under this paragraph shall be served simultaneously on the other party, except as provided in paragraph 7. If a party withholds information pursuant to paragraph 7, that party will inform the other party and the arbitrator of the nature of the information that has been withheld.
5. The arbitrator shall hold a fact-finding conference within 90 days of the filing of the initial response, unless there is good cause for a delay. A claimant may represent him or herself at the fact-finding conference and at all other stages of the proceedings or may be represented by the Guild, or by an individual or counsel of the claimant's own choosing. The Post
- 3 -
p. 4 content martha11-pg4.jpeg
Ashley's file: Arbitation Procedures pg 4.jpeg · replaces B32F08FD-3861-416B-A4F6-D6671C5AAF7B_1_105_c.jpeg
[Superseded scan was photographed sideways; this image is upright. Page opens mid-paragraph:]
'...may be represented by counsel with other representative[s] of its own choosing.
6. Each party shall be given an opportunity at the beginning of the fact-finding conference to state its position, directly or through counsel or a representative. At the request of either party or on his or her own initiative, the arbitrator may request the presence of witnesses at the fact-finding conference. Witnesses will testify under oath and may be sequestered by the arbitrator. Parties or their counsel or representative may suggest questions to be put to a witness, but all questioning of witnesses shall be by the arbitrator.
7. Under no circumstances shall a claimant or witness be given access to name-linked salary or name-linked compensation information or to performance evaluations of other employees. In addition, other confidential information shall not be disclosed to a party or witness except to the extent that the arbitrator determines that such disclosure of such other confidential information is essential to a fair adjudication of the claim, and that there is no reasonable alternative (including, for example, redaction, preparation of summaries or general descriptions, or the use of compiled or generalized data rather than individualized data). When the arbitrator determines that such other confidential information must be made available to a party or to a witness, the arbitrator shall order the party or witness not to disclose such information to any third other than his or her counsel or representative, and the party or witness
- 4 -'
p. 5 content 44F2AF4E-0809-4164-9C8E-901D1834A786_1_105_c.jpeg
Ashley's file: Arbitation Procedures pg 5.jpeg
[Page photographed sideways — rotate 90° counter-clockwise to read. Continuation of prior paragraph:]
shall be required, as a condition to receiving such information, to sign an acknowledgement stating that he or she has received confidential information and agreeing that he or she shall not disclose that information except to his or her counsel or representative, who shall sign a similar acknowledgement. (Nothing in this paragraph shall constitute an admission by the Guild that it is not entitled to name-linked salary or name-linked compensation information or performance evaluations of employees or to other "confidential" information in a proceeding not governed by these procedures.)
8. The arbitrator may schedule such additional sessions of the fact-finding conference as he/she deems appropriate. At the conclusion of the fact-finding conference, the parties will be given an opportunity to discuss settlement. Absent agreement by the parties, the arbitrator shall not participate in settlement discussions. If the parties do not agree on a settlement, the parties shall be given an opportunity to submit additional information to the arbitrator. Such additional information shall be provided to the other party, consistent with the provisions of paragraph 7, and the other party shall have an opportunity to respond. The arbitrator shall then issue a written decision, with reasons, that shall be final and binding on the parties. No decision or settlement arrived at under these procedures shall be considered as evidence or precedent in any other proceeding.
- 5 -
p. 6 content martha11-pg6.jpeg
Ashley's file: Arbitation Procedures pg 6.jpeg · replaces 3B9C8F5A-5ECE-42F7-AD0E-26C09181A298_1_105_c.jpeg
[Superseded scan was photographed sideways; this image is upright.]
9. The arbitrator's determination shall be limited to a determination of whether the Post engaged in discrimination on the basis of gender, race and/or Hispanic origin in compensation under Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964. In the event of a finding of discrimination, the arbitrator may award back pay dating to the date of the discrimination, but no earlier than September 1, 1994, and may order prospective adjustment in compensation. No other relief may be awarded.
10. The Post and the claimant (or the Guild when it represents the claimant) shall bear their own costs and attorney's fees. In cases in which the Guild represents the claimant, the Guild and the Post shall split the fees and costs of the arbitrator. In all other cases, the individual claimant and the Post shall split the fees and costs of the arbitrator.
11. In cases in which the Guild does not represent the claimant, it shall be provided with a copy of the claim and decision and, unless the claimant objects, may attend the fact-finding conference.
12. Consistent with the Post's general policy against retaliation and the requirements of law, employees will not be subject to retaliation for participating in a proceeding under these provisions.
13. The time limits established by these Arbitration Procedures may be extended by agreement or upon the approval of the arbitrator.

CONFIDENTIAL INFORMATION UNDER THE AGREED ARBITRATION PROCEDURES
PREPARED FOR SETTLEMENT PURPOSES
Listed below is a comparison of the salaries of certain male reporters to the salaries of Martha Hamilton, Caroline Mayer, Cynthia Skrzycki and Sharon Walsh. Relevant background information for each male reporter is also included. Lastly, explanations are given - where appropriate - noting the factors that have influenced that reporter's salary. Where subjective assessments are given, they reflect the views expressed by the relevant Assistant Managing Editors.
In comparing individual salaries, The Post has not stated the exact salary differential, but rather noted the degree to which an individual is paid more or less than the respective claimant. The following key is instructive.
If a reporter was paid similarly, his salary was within $1,000, more or less, of the claimant. If a reporter was paid slightly more, his salary was between $1,001 and $5,000 greater than the salary of the claimant. If a reporter was paid more, his salary was between $5,001 and $15,000 greater than the salary of the claimant. If a reporter was paid substantially more, his salary was over $15,000 greater than the salary of the claimant. The same wording is used to describe a male reporter whose salary was less than the claimant.
Keith Alexander
Keith Alexander was paid slightly more than Hamilton on the 2001 and 2002 salary snapshots.
Experience and Education
Alexander came to The Post recently as a reporter in Financial News. Prior to starting at The Post, Alexander worked as a reporter at USA Today for approximately five and-a-half years. He also spent approximately two years as a reporter at Business Week and approximately two years as a reporter at the Dayton Daily News. Alexander is a graduate of Howard University.
Starting Salary
The Post cannot locate information about Alexander's salary at the USA Today. His starting salary was slightly more than the salary of Hamilton at that time.
Salary History
As of September 1, 2002, Alexander has not received any merit increases.
Explanation
The differential between the salary of Alexander and the salary of Hamilton is due to Alexander's starting salary, which reflects The Post's desire to hire him away

Upon starting at The Post, Yang received approximately a 14 percent increase over his prior salary. His starting salary was slightly less than the salary of Skrzycki at that time.
Salary History
Yang received one merit increase during his time at The Post - which he received as a reporter in National. Yang received a salary increase when he became an Assignment Editor in Financial News in 1992. He also received a salary increase when he became Director of Recruiting in 1999.
Explanation:
Yang previously held key positions at The Post, such as White House correspondent, resulting in a merit increase while he was in National. He came to Financial News as an Assignment Editor. He retained his editor's salary when he became a reporter in Financial News.

Post Unit News
[Newspaper Guild logo]
Washington/Baltimore Newspaper Guild
No. 99 Date October 22, 1987
Discrimination at The Post
PAY GAPS WIDEN
The patterns of pay discrimination that have contributed over the years to low morale and wide gaps in the amount Washington Post managers are willing to pay their employees not only persist, but have widened significantly since management imposed its version of the contract in August.
If you needed a reason to understand why this contract is inadequate, here it is.
Last we looked, white males topped the salary scales in the best-paying jobs in many of the largest job categories at the Post. The news is this: despite the company's stated commitments to the hiring and nurturing of women and minorities, the practice of paying inequitably based on race and sex has not only continued--it's gotten worse.
For reporters--the largest single job category covered by the contract--the average salary for white males was $988.68 at the beginning of 1986. By September of 1987, that figure had jumped to $1,140.52.
Such a salary improvement is not the source of our complaints, however. The problem is that while white males were continuing to improve, females and blacks continued the slide away from parity.
The numbers compiled after the so-called impasse was declared in mid-August tell the story.
Across all classifications, white males average $332 more a week than black females, $204.39 more than white females and $230.66 more than black males.
Among reporters, the figures are reversed slightly, but the discrepancies remain just as stark. White male reporters, on the average, make $240.59 more a week than black females, a gap that is $43.24 wider than in January of 1986.
The gap has also grown by about $2 a week between white male and white female reporters and by about $15 a week between white male and black male reporters.
Among outside advertising salespersons, the gap also increased significantly. White males, on average, earned about $25 more a week than black males and black females in 1986, a disparity which has now increased to $96 weekly. White females in this classification earn on average $61.51 less than their male counterparts, up from a $43.03 average weekly difference in 1986.
And the gap among white men and white women who work as T-5 Data Programmers has opened dramatically, moving from an $8.33 average weekly difference in 1986 to an $86.07 average difference now.
There have been some improvements. Black females who are classified as managers, assistant managers, editors, deputy and associate editors have closed a more than $200 gap between their average salaries and salaries for white males.
But overall, the Post's record on equal pay for equal work sends a clear message to the rank and file at the Washington Post about which employees are valued at this company, and which are not.
These comparisons tell the story:
Reporters 1/86 9/87
-over-
Affiliated with American Federation of Labor—Congress of [Industrial Organizations — footer cut off]

Coverage of the 1980 EEO sex-discrimination settlement

newsletter-clipping not-asserted medium order high read 1980 2 pages

Subject: Post Unit News reporting on the settlement of the 1972 class sex-discrimination complaint, including the Special Bulletin of 2 December 1980.

Notes: Thematic pairing of two newsletter pages about the same settlement. Order not asserted.

p. 1 content 36C7EEB3-BA04-42D6-9DCC-CBF49EB5CCA4_1_105_c.jpeg
as of November 29, 1979. The three scholarship recipients will each receive a $5,000 stipend and the Post will pay for their tuition, fees and books. The two sabbatical recipients will receive half of their current salary; the Post will pay for tuition, fees and books.
• Sixty days after the signing of the agreement, the Post will name a manager of employment and affirmative action, who will meet weekly with the personnel director, the publisher and the general manager. Department heads will report quarterly on their progress in achieving the goals of the agreement.
• Annual evaluations of vice presidents and department heads will be "based in part on their efforts to achieve the goals established by this agreement, and their eligibility for promotions, bonuses and salary increases will be based in part on their efforts in this area."
• A female employee who has a complaint about implementation of the agreement should first discuss it with her immediate supervisor. If it is not resolved, she may proceed to the assistant managing editor for personnel, the manager of employment and affirmative action, the personnel director or the publisher. She may have another employee accompany her.
• No employee is precluded from taking new complaints of sex discrimination, including complaint of sexual harassment, directly to the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission.
• On May 1 and November 1 of each of the next five years, the Post will send a report on its affirmative action to the EEOC; to Linda Singer, the attorney who represented the Post women; and to Alan Eisenberg, attorney for the Guild. The report will detail the number of men and women working at each classification of job covered by the agreement; the number of vacancies filled and the sex of the person selected; the number of applicants and their sex; the number of transfer and promotion requests received and the sex of the applicants; the number of persons by sex given new jobs or who left the Post; a list of jobs for which no notice was posted.
• The Post is also paying all fees and expenses involved in the case — including up to $42,500 for the lawyers representing the women and the Guild.
A committee of Guild members at the Post will be monitoring the efforts of the newspaper to reach the goals of the agreement. It will be available to help any woman who wants advice or assistance or who has a problem on the job. For answers to your questions about the agreement, call Claudia Levy, ext. 7110, or Jane Huffman, ext. 7157.
Claudia Levy for the Washington Post Unit
Ernie Mercer, Unit Chair
Eric Geist, Commercial Vice Chair
Joanne Omang, Editorial Vice Chair
Mary Williams, Secretary
Jane Huffman, Chief Shop Steward

Post Unit News
[AFL-CIO seal] Washington/Baltimore Newspaper Guild
No. 18 Date December 2, 1980
SPECIAL BULLETIN — EEO SETTLEMENT
Within the next month, our lawyers will be asking Guild employees who signed a class action sex discrimination complaint filed against the newspaper eight years ago to sign off on a settlement with the Post announced last week. The agreement, to be monitored by the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission, does not go into effect until the more than 100 charging parties say they accept it.
Persons who filed the complaint are urged to sign the agreement as soon as they are contacted. Once the agreement is formalized, our lawyers will have three months to try to contact and obtain signed releases from members of the class - the more than 550 women who were working at the Post at least six months between May 29, 1972, when the complaint was filed, and June 19, 1974, when the EEOC found that the complaint was justified. If you worked here at that period, whether or not you signed the complaint, you are a member of the class. Releases from 95 per cent of those who can be located have to be returned before the agreement goes into effect.
Women who sign releases will be eligible for the following compensation, depending on how long they were employed:
up to 1 year.....$ 50
1 to 2 years.....100
2 to 3 years.....175
3 to 4 years.....250
Summary of the agreement and copies of the entire settlement are being mailed to charging parties. Here are some other provisions:
* The Post will make a "good faith effort" over the next five years to fill one-third of the officials, managers and professional jobs in news and editorial that become available with women. In some departments, the goals will be different: Metro's goal is 40 per cent of the jobs that open up; Sports, 20 per cent; Style, 40 per cent. A third of all assignment editor, critic and columnist jobs, excluding copy editors, are to be offered to women.
* This same "good faith effort" will be made to fill a third of the job openings for managers in combined commercial departments with women. The specific goal for officials and managers in Accounting is 35 per cent; Administration, 25 per cent; Advertising, 33.3 per cent; Circulation, 15 per cent. One-third of the professional jobs opening up in commercial are to go to women, with goals of 35 per cent for Administration and Advertising. In addition, a goal of 45 per cent is set for outside sales persons in Advertising.
* Job vacancies will be posted at the employment office and on bulletin boards. Any employee denied a vacant position can ask to be given the reasons and the steps needed to qualify - in writing.
* A woman who believes that her title and salary do not compensate her for her duties and responsibilities may request a change in classification and salary within 30 days after the effective date of the agreement. She may make this request directly to Publisher Donald Graham, or, if she prefers, to the personnel director, the manager of employment and affirmative action or the assistant managing editor for personnel, who shall make recommendations to the publisher. The employee may have another employee accompany her. If the request is in writing, the response will be in writing. We will have more about this in a later bulletin.
* The Post says it will "expand its general training and career development activities in all Commercial departments" over the next five years and make them "available to all women on a nondiscriminatory basis." The current summer and two-year internship programs in the newsroom will also continue, with 40 per cent of the positions aimed at women.
* During the next five years, the Post will offer three scholarships and two sabbaticals to women employees for'up to one year, to study journalism, business, commercial art or data processing. The women have to have been employed at the Post
(more)

Maternity and sick leave: 1976 request and contract provisions

MEMORANDUM
May 4, 1976
FOR: Bob Derby
FROM: Natalie Panetti
RE: Martha Hamilton - Maternity Leave/Sick Leave Request
This is evidently not a new issue. I've attached a copy
of the Guild Contract (Page 34, 4a and 4b) language
regarding Maternity Leave. Also attached is page 29 &
30 regarding Sick Leave (Article X as referred to in 4b).
This is definitely confusing.
Larry Wallace is out till Thursday, but I was able to
talk to Jack Pierce. According to Jack, The Post has
taken the position that:
1) If a person, prior to the birth of a child and while
still an active employee, becomes ill for any reason,
she may avail herself of any sick leave benefits due
her.
2) Once an employee goes on leave for maternity reasons
she is no longer eligible for sick leave--until she
again becomes an active employee.
3) In actuality, according to Jack, an employee is
eligible for six months leave of absence for maternity
reasons. The four weeks is strictly a guide to deter-
mine the dollar amount an eligible person might receive
as a maternity benefit.

such substitute employees voluntarily resigning shall be paid severance in accordance with Article XIII of this Agreement.
(4)(c) Employees discharged for economy reasons, but not those employees whose substitute voluntary resignations are accepted by The Post, shall be placed on a rehiring list for a period of two (2) years and shall be rehired according to seniority, for the same or comparable jobs when such openings occur.
(5) In the event that any fulltime or regular part-time employee is displaced because of the adoption of improved methods or the installation of new equipment, or the use of a new process, such employee will be given, wherever practicable, the opportunity to be retrained at The Post's expense to meet The Post's needs for employment resulting from either new job opportunities or those that result from normal turnover, or given first consideration for an open position in a comparable classification for which he/she qualifies, or dismissed in accordance with the provisions of subparagraphs (4)(a) and (b) above.
(6) Upon notice of discharge, or upon discharge where no notice is given, an employee who makes request within ten (10) days shall receive from The Post a written statement of the cause of his discharge, with a copy of such statement furnished to the Guild.
ARTICLE X - SICK LEAVE
(1) In case of bona fide illness, unless clearly arising from his own misconduct, an employee shall be entitled, in each twelve-month period, three (3) weeks' sick leave. For the purpose of this paragraph, absence during working hours due to emergency sickness, psychiatric, medical or dental care, shall be considered a bona fide illness where it is not possible for the employee to schedule such emergency care during non-working hours. Any such sick leave not exhausted by an employee during the twelve-month period to which it relates,
29
p. 3 content 845A7ECA-10AE-4F48-89A5-CF32D47A6080_1_105_c.jpeg
may, in case of bona fide illness not arising from his own misconduct, be used during the next succeeding period of thirty-six (36) months (employees with at least five (5) but less than ten (10) years), fifteen (15) days per year carried over to the next succeeding forty-eight (48) months; employees with more than ten (10) years of service but less than twenty (20) years, fifteen (15) days per year carried over to the next succeeding sixty (60) months; employees with twenty (20) years of service or more, fifteen (15) days per year carried over to the next succeeding ninety-six (96) months). Additional sick leave may be granted at the discretion of The Post. No deductions shall be made on account of sick leave from overtime credited or to be credited to an employee or from annual vacations. An employee shall be entitled to four (4) days' sick leave during the first three (3) months of his employment.
(2) The Post may in any case require an employee to submit a certificate of a reputable physician, or may have him examined by a physician of its own selection, in order to determine whether such employee is entitled to sick leave hereunder.
(3) Any amounts received by an employee under any Workmen's Compensation law (except hospital and medical expenses) for the period covered by sick leave may be deducted by The Post.
ARTICLE XI - VACATIONS
(1) For the purpose of this Article, "vacation" shall mean vacation with pay, "employment" shall mean regular, fulltime continuous employment at The Post, and "working days" shall mean days worked for straight-time pay and paid sick leave, holidays and vacations.
(2) For the purpose of this Article, employees are divided into two groups:
30
p. 4 content 31969D00-7026-463D-BC22-B1A085F4B117_1_105_c.jpeg
(4)(a) Maternity leave of at least six (6) months shall be granted with pay for the first four (4) weeks if an employee has been continuously in the employ of The Post for one (1) year or more. Work which may be performed at home may be offered to the employee and compensated at the daily straight-time rate, and any days worked at home shall not reduce or extend the period of leave.
(4)(b) The decision as to when such leave shall commence, shall reside solely with the employee with the concurrence of her own physician. Prior to the commencement of maternity leave, the employee may avail herself of any sick leave benefit to which she is entitled under the provisions of Article X. Any unused sick leave is available to her upon her return to work.
(4)(c) Paternity leave of up to ten (10) days shall be granted without pay upon request. Such leave shall be requested in writing by the employee.
(5) Leaves of absence under this Article or under Article XVII shall not constitute breaks in continuity of service but except as otherwise provided in Article XVII, the time spent on such leaves shall not be considered service time.
(6) When an employee is elected to an office of the Washington-Baltimore Newspaper Guild, or as a member of the Washington-Baltimore Newspaper Guild Executive Board, or as a delegate to the Greater Washington Central Labor Council, he may request that his regular time off under Article VI of this Agreement, coincide with the regular meetings of those organizations. The Post will grant such request where it is not in conflict with the operating requirements of the Company.
(7) During the term of this agreement, four (4) employees with seven (7) or more years of service shall be granted sabbatical leaves of nine (9) months, for which period the employee will be paid at one-half the applicable contract

Post-Guild joint EEO statement, claims procedure and Task Force announcement

arbitration-exhibit content-sequence medium order low read 1987-1997 5 pages

Subject: The joint statement of the Post and the Guild on equal employment opportunity, the arbitration claims procedure, and the announcement of the Task Force on personnel practices.

Notes: Sequence follows the life of one agreement: the cover and joint statement, then the claims procedure and Task Force memo, then the final agreement page of 3 October 1997.

p. 1 partial 42E20208-28B6-4EBC-9155-B694160374FA_1_105_c.jpeg

TO EQUALITY [?] ...' and a lower line reading '...COMMITMENT TO EQUAL EMPLOYMENT'. A date line near the bottom left of center appears to read 'Date: October 3, 198?', but the year digit is uncertain. p. 2 content 4AFB1E91-E965-4F67-BAE9-98DCD01D4697_1_105_c.jpeg
Joint Statement of The Washington Post and
the Washington-Baltimore Newspaper Guild
The Washington Post and the Washington-Baltimore Newspaper Guild have agreed to resolve a charge of discrimination that was filed by the Guild in 1988 with the District of Columbia Office of Human Rights and the U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission. The charge was disputed by the Post and investigated by the Office of Human Rights until 1994, when the EEOC assumed jurisdiction. As a result of the settlement agreement, the EEOC has terminated its investigation, without reaching any findings, and approved the settlement agreement between the Post and the Guild. The principal provisions of the agreement are as follows:
• The agreement creates a procedure for final and binding arbitration that may be used by current employees who wish to assert a claim of discrimination on the basis of gender, race [i.e., Black, White, Asian, Native American] and/or Hispanic origin in wages or other compensation while in a Guild-covered position. The procedure is limited to claims of discrimination in compensation during the period beginning September 1, 1994. If the alleged discrimination is claimed to have begun before that date and continued after that date, a claim may be asserted, but any award of back pay would be limited to the period beginning September 1, 1994. Arbitrators will also be authorized to provide prospective adjustments in pay upon a finding of unlawful discrimination. The Post and the Guild will select arbitrators with experience in handling employment discrimination matters.
p. 3 content 895DDE75-2A37-45EE-85A3-D2EF04D561CD_1_105_c.jpeg
Claims must be filed by December 2, 1997. Claim forms
and a copy of the arbitration procedures are available from the
Personnel Department (202-334-7174) or the Guild (202-393-0808).
Anyone considering filing a claim should examine the claim form
and the arbitration procedures carefully. They contain important
information concerning such matters as: the kinds of claims that
will be considered, the information that should be provided to
pursue a claim, the steps that the arbitrator will follow in
resolving a claim, the party's options with respect to
representation, and the payment of the costs and fees of the
arbitrator. The Guild will provide representation to anyone who
requests it, in which case the Guild will pay the claimant's
share (50%) of the arbitrator's fee and costs.
Consistent with its general policy and with the
requirements of law, the Post will not retaliate against any
employee who participates in an arbitration proceeding.
• The agreement calls for the establishment of a
Task Force to examine personnel practices for Guild-covered
positions in the commercial departments. The Task Force, to be
formed in January 1998, will look at such issues as hiring,
promotion, training, communications, diversity, career
development and performance evaluation. It will be chaired and
appointed by the Director of Personnel, Carl Williams, and will
include at least 15 employees from the commercial departments, no
more than half of whom will be supervisors or managers. All
commercial department employees are encouraged to volunteer their
