Letter from Black female reporters of The Washington Post on salary disparities

letter content-sequence medium order medium read 1967 / early 1980s 2 pages

Subject: Signed letter from the Black female reporters of The Washington Post to 'Leonard' setting out the salary disparities they had identified.

Notes: Two pages of one letter: the opening page followed by the signed closing page.

p. 1 content BF7FD77A-6DA0-48F7-BA5C-B66CB2ABB71C_1_105_c.jpeg
The Washington Post
1150 15TH STREET, N.W.
WASHINGTON, D.C. 20071
(202) 334-6000
WRITER'S DIRECT TELEPHONE NUMBER [blank]
Jan. 2, 1967
Dear Leonard:
As black women working at the Washington Post, we are deeply concerned about the disparities between our salaries and those of our colleagues.
During a recent WDOL-AM radio show, Don Graham stated that the average salary for a Washington Post reporter is $50,000 per year. As far as we can determine, only two of 17 black females in the newsroom receive salaries at or above that level.
As you are aware, the Guild's report on salary differences found that black female reporters receive an average of $791.33 per week compared to averages of $986.68 for white males, $920.46 for black males and $859.37 for white females.
While we are concerned about the salary gaps that such statistics reveal between white males and all other employees and between men and women, we wish to address the salary levels of black women because clearly we are at the bottom.
Graham's statement about average salaries coupled with the Guild report has made it clear that black women are the least valued employees in the newsroom. At least five earn less than the $791.33 weekly average. We are also concerned that the merit system is not applied in an evenhanded manner.
As Post employees, we feel that we are not being treated fairly. Whether our salaries are part of a pattern of discrimination, as the Guild maintained in its report, or the unintended impact of a dual tier pay system, the result is the same. Black women are the lowest paid in the newsroom. Using the average weekly salaries, the gap between black women and white males, for example, is $10,262.20 a year.
p. 2 content 7D8E158D-5627-43FF-9857-D719C5339A4A_1_105_c.jpeg
We strongly believe that something must be done to correct this situation. During at least one meeting with staff members, you indicated that you would be evaluating the salary situation and addressing past inequities. We support such an effort.
In addition, we have two requests:
*That the salaries of black female employees be adjusted to levels that equal the salaries of white male employees who have the same amount of experience and are in comparable positions.
*That newly hired black female employees who are fully experienced receive salaries equal to those of newly hired white males who are fully experienced.
We make these requests in a sincere effort to end the financial inequity for those of us who are black and female. We look forward to your response.
Sincerely,
[handwritten signatures with figures, mostly hard to read:]

Black women are devastated by both race and sex discrimination. On average, black women reporters earn $172.00 a week less than white males of similar age and tenure. Of all other workers who are not reporters, black women earn an average of $212.00 less than white men.
4. That The Post relies on subjective judgment in setting merit pay. Its failure to establish objective, nondiscriminatory job-related criteria has led to fewer and lesser merit increases for women, blacks and other minorities.
The Guild has attempted to address the issue of discrimination with Post management, to no avail. We have tried to set our house in order. The Post has refused to cooperate.
Today, we have asked for assistance from the D.C. Office of Human Rights. This office has the authority to subpoena information from management, the same information management has consistently refused to release to the Guild. This office will investigate the charges, attempt conciliation if they are substantiated and, if conciliation fails, arrange a hearing before the D.C. Commission on Human Rights. (If you have information which might interest the Office of Human Rights, you can contact attorney/investigator Jeffery Tacy directly at (202) 939-8740.)
In May of this year, Guild members voted overwhelmingly to file the discrimination charge. We are confident that the elimination of discrimination at The Post will benefit everyone who works at The Post. Creating an environment in which all workers are treated fairly will inevitably make this great paper a greater one. We look forward to the day when we will be judged by the content of our work and not by our skin, age or sex.
THE MESSAGE IS CLEAR!
Thanks to all who despite management's attempts to make the last two days agonizing and unpleasant for many ---oops! Thanks to all of you who participated in the byline strike, a rousing success, contrary to management's mixed messages.
Withholding your byline sends a message not only to management, but to other workers and our readership that all is not well at The Washington Post.
Employees in the Commercial Dept. are circulating a petition urging management to "return to the bargaining table and negotiate in good faith so that we can all enjoy a fair contract and a more harmonious workplace." We appreciate their support. We will attempt to present the petition to Do[n] Graham today.
-- Jill Neilson, Unit Chair

Post Unit News
Washington/Baltimore Newspaper Guild No. 21 Date July 13, 1998
GUILD FILES DISCRIMINATION COMPLAINT
Today The Newspaper Guild filed a class action complaint on behalf of our 1,400 members with the D.C. office of Human Rights charging The Washington Post with discrimination on the basis of age, race, national origin and sex.
This action was taken after Guild efforts to persuade management to eliminate discrimination in this shop had failed. The Post has consistently refused to give the Guild definitive information concerning our members, or to discuss the issue of discrimination with us.
It is not an action taken in haste, but after two years without a contract; three years of the Post's refusal to release salary data to the Guild; and a constant refusal on the part of management to even discuss discrimination in compensation at The Post.
Some of the points of the charge:
1. That The Post discriminates on the basis of sex:
- Female reporters at The Post on average earn $121.00 a week less than men - even when their salaries are adjusted for age and tenure.
- Failing to hire and retain qualified women in proportion to their representation in the community. Women are 57% of The Post's work force, yet only one out of five editors or managers is a woman.
- Segregating women in certain lower-paid classifications. 61% of the office and clerical workers employed by The Post are women.
2. That The Post discriminates on the basis of age by:
"...deliberately giving poor evaluations of present and potential performance to older employees by failing to reward older employees commensurately with younger employees; by denying older employees training offered to younger employees by assigning older employees to less desirable positions than younger employees; by promoting younger employees more frequently and to better positions than older employees; and by terminating older employees, and coercing them to leave The Post."
3. That The Post discriminates on the basis of race by:
- Failing to pay blacks and other minorities as much as whites for substantially equivalent work. Black reporters on average earn $31.00 less per week than whites. Other black employees on average make $200.00 less than white employees at The Post.
- Failure to hire blacks, Hispanics and other minorities in proportion to their representation in the community. Hispanics comprise only 1% of The Post workforce, according to the most recent data made available by The Post to The Guild.
(over)
Affiliated with American Federation of Labor-Congress of Industrial Organizations, Canadian Labour Congress.

Maternity-leave grievance and settlement correspondence, 1976-1978

letter editorial-chronological medium order high read 1976-04-29 to 1978-12-12 3 pages

Subject: Hamilton's request for six weeks sick leave after maternity, the union's reply on the grievance, and the letter enclosing the arbitration settlement cheque.

Notes: Ordered by the dates on the letters, which trace the grievance from the request through settlement.

p. 1 content 4E570377-5FC9-4D22-80B7-21B6ECAFF506_1_105_c.jpeg
[red exhibit banner '6' on left and right margins]
To: Bob Baker, Elsie Carper
From: Martha M. Hamilton
4/29/76
This is to request six weeks sick leave after I have my baby (about May 15). According to my doctor, this is the length of time for which I will be physically unable to return to work.
The contract provides for one month's paid maternity leave, which, as I understand it, should be paid in addition to sick leave and accrued vacation time. My understanding of maternity leave is that it is provided for social purposes, to allow a new mother to arrange for child care and to make other adjustments required by the birth of a child. The fact that maternity leave and sick leave are different provisions in our contract^[a] clearly indicates they are to serve different purposes. So does section XII, 4 (b) which says, in part, "Prior to the commencement of maternity leave, the employee may avail herself of any sick leave benefit to which she is entitled...."
Thanks,
[signed in ink:] Martha Hamilton
p. 2 partial E47BB939-C1A1-4460-AE48-B088330E4A1D_1_105_c.jpeg
-3-
was a recess of the hearing, during which the two parties discussed the grievance and a possible settlement. It was finally agreed that that particular grievance, which involved you, would be settled on the basis of The Post paying you six weeks of pay at your salary rate at the time you went on maternity leave.
The Guild reserved its right to file a new grievance in respect to this subject at any time in the future. The Post preserved its position that the language of the old, expired contract did not require The Post to grant paid sick leave when an employee was on maternity leave.
I want to make it clear that The Post agreed to pay this money to you simply to avert what appeared would be a complicated, protracted arbitration hearing, and to obviate the necessity of spending many, many hours, much effort, and considerable expense on the arbitration.
I want to make it clear that this money does not represent the payment of six weeks of sick leave. That is to say, six weeks will not be deducted from your accrued sick leave.
Very truly yours,
[signed]
Lawrence A. Wallace
cc: Robt. Paul, Esq.
D. Struzinski, NDNG 735
bcc: Peggy Powers ✓
Elsie Carper ✓

December 12, 1978
Mrs. Martha Hamilton
Newsroom
Washington Post
Dear Mrs. Hamilton:
Enclosed is Washington Post check number 950540 in the amount of $2,117.85.
Allow me to explain why we are giving you this check.
Several weeks ago there was an arbitration hearing scheduled with the Guild. The Guild's grievance was whether or not the language of the old, expired contract between The Post and the Guild required The Post to allow employees going on maternity leave to use some, or all, of their accrued sick leave while they were on maternity leave. The position of The Post was that the plain language of the contract did not require that, that was not the intent of the language, and, further, a consistent past practice had been not to grant sick leave to employees while they were on maternity leave. To put it another way, sick leave and maternity leave are two entirely different kinds of leave, and the language did not require that these two kinds of benefits could be used together.
After the arbitration hearing had been under way about an hour or so, it became obvious that the hearing was going to be protracted, confused, complicated, etc. At that point, there [continues]

Wage-discrimination arbitration claim forms

arbitration-exhibit content-sequence medium order medium read 1997 3 pages

Subject: The GBA/Post settlement arbitration claim form and the gender/race wage-discrimination questionnaire completed and signed by Martha Hamilton.

Notes: Sequence follows the claim: blank procedure form, then the signed final page of 12 October 1997, then page 1 of the questionnaire. Page 2 of that questionnaire is in Martha 17.

CLAIM FORM
Under a settlement agreement between the Washington-Baltimore Newspaper Guild and the Washington Post, a procedure for final and binding arbitration has been created to hear certain claims of alleged discrimination in wages or other compensation.
To be eligible to file a claim:
- You must be a current employee of The Washington Post.
- You must currently, or you must have at some period between September 1, 1994 and the present, been employed in a Guild-covered position at the Post.
- You must sign a statement that you believe in good faith that you were discriminated against on the basis of your gender, race and/or Hispanic origin in connection with your wages or other compensation in a Guild-covered position at the Post at some time between September 1, 1994 and the present. If the alleged discrimination is claimed to have begun before September 1, 1994, a claim may be asserted as long as the discrimination continued after that date.
- You must abide by the procedures applicable to the arbitration process. Those procedures are attached to this Claim Form.
- The Claim Form must be delivered, or postmarked for delivery, to Carol Melamed, Vice President Government Affairs, The Washington Post, 1150 15th Street, N.W., Washington, D.C. 20071 by December 2, 1997.
- You must agree to be bound by the decision of the arbitrator.
Please answer the questions on this Claim Form to the best of your ability. If you have any questions, please call the Guild (at 202-393-0808) or Carol Melamed (at 202-334-6105). If necessary, to complete your answers, use the other side of the form or additional pieces of paper. Consistent with the Post's policy against retaliation and the requirements of law, employees will not be subject to retaliation for filing a claim or participating in a proceeding.

Draft memo to 'Leonard': proposals for education coverage

memo internal-pagination (3 of 4 pages) mixed order low read undated (1980s-90s) 4 pages

Subject: Hamilton's typescript draft memo pitching ideas for education journalism, with her handwritten notes.

Notes: One memo recovered from four fragments. Pages 1-3 follow the document's own page numbering and the closing page is signed; the handwritten notes page is placed last, which is our choice rather than the document's, so its position is not asserted. Three of these pages are Ashley's higher-resolution re-scans.

p. 1 content 5637EF0F-E28D-4ED2-9CA0-BDB970DD3F07_1_105_c.jpeg
[handwritten top right: 17]
Leonard --
I think our metropolitan area coverage of education is fine as far as it goes, but I don't think it goes far enough.
One whole type of stories we could do more with is ~~education as big business~~ For instance:
-- the selling of a textbook. Textbooks are big business, and getting one adopted by a major school systems means big money. That's what made Paul Samuleson a rich man. Stories we could do ~~include~~ [handwritten insertion: an overview of the economics of the business, ] what the current bestsellers are and the process [struck word] of making them into bestsellers -- promotion including bookfairs and wooing high school english teachers on the selection committees. We might be able to find a local textbook author and write about how a grammar text written over many summers has provided a nest egg for retirement. It would be interesting, too, to find out in how many systems Roots is now text.
-- subliminal advertising in the schools. A D.C. school board member once mentioned the number of freebies that the school system gets from major corporations -- films, pencils, book covers -- I'm not sure what types of items there are. The "Stick-Up-For-Breakfast" stuff that the D.C. schools used included pictures of Tony the Tiger, a Kellogg trademark. I would guess that most corporate-supplied material contains a plug for the corporation's product. There may be nothing wrong with it. The schools may come out winners because of money saved by not having to buy some item. But it would be a good story to know how much of this goes on and in what fashion.
[handproof marks and a downward arrow at foot of page]
p. 2 content 2D71B907-B490-4441-8369-5644F2D69D62_1_105_c.jpeg
memo/2
-- the testing business. _v Starting with a figure on how much an ambitious high school senior may spend on pre-college testing and trans^cripts, a look at the testing empire would be a good story, including looking at smaller testing outfits that may want to challenge Educational Testing ^Service, which has a lock on the market.
-- the higher education money managers, the people charged with making endowments grow.
That's one type of story. The Wall Street Journal has done some gcod stories -- such as a recent one about middle class f^milies being almost priced out of ^ higher education -- just by looking at the financial side of education.
There are other stories as well!
-- As I mentioned last October, I think the ^b_ story of how the Viet Nam war is being taught and finding the veterans of the war (the Rostows, Rusks, and peace protest organizers) who now may be teaching it, is a good story.
-- So too, is the question of social promotion. Bart had a story about a West Virginia town where they have ceased pro^oting kids who don't master certain skills. I once started looking into this question for Kevin and was struck by research I read that showed that the largest incidence of failure is among male first grade students. The research suggested kids were failing for behavioral reasons as much as anyth^ing. Girls who sat quietly passed; boys who bounced around the room didn'^t. More rese^arch indicated that kids who fail show less mastery after repeating a grade than they did the first time.
[v]
p. 3 content EDB712D1-FAE5-4441-9639-EE6CF1B66050_1_105_c.jpeg
memo/3
* There's probably research that demonstrates the opposite, too. It's an intriguing question. (Maybe we could do a sidebar on the common middle class dream about failure -- the one where you walk into class on the day of the big test and realize you haven't been to class all year. School must breed a fine fear of failure into people.)
Any of these stories could be written well from a metropolitan [word struck through] perspective.
Before, I was a general assignments reporter covering the D.C. schools. We had the Education Bureau sitting off in a corner, and I was circumscribed by jurisdictional problems.[strikethrough] I liked what * I did, as far as it went, but it left me interested in doing more -- in covering education as we cover medicine and have sometimes covered housing and other issues.
If you're interested, let's talk about ways I might move in this direction.
[handwritten signature] Martha Hamilton

Post Unit News
[AFL-CIO seal] Washington/Baltimore Newspaper Guild
No. 103 -/ Date December 3, 198[7]
EQUALITY AND JUSTICE, POST STYLE
Post Unit News seems to have nothing but readers whenever we put together a listing of Post salaries. Last May we told you about salaries for full-time employees covered by the Guild contract as of October, 1986. Now we've got similar data as of September's payroll-- after the company illegally declared an impasse in contract talks and unilaterally imposed its final wage offer.
As you can see, the latest raises have only widened the pay disparity for people doing the same jobs. This enormous range of salaries shows all the more how subjective the system of compensation has become during the years since The Post instituted a dual pay system here.
Some of the higher-paid employees at The Post have climbed the salary list through longevity, as beneficiaries of general pay increases handed out to the workforce over the years but not applied to the contract minimums. Others among the higher-paid are newer, so-called "fast track" employees who have been given merit raises to surpass what many of their colleagues are earning. Women and black employees do much worse than others, on average.
The lower-paid employees in any job classification are the real victims of The Post's unfair pay system. They earn less not through any fault or failure on their part, but because of the unfair, discriminatory system imposed by the publisher a decade ago to boost profits. The result, an absurd array of pay for the same jobs, is printed below.
Affiliated with American Federation of labor-Congress of Industrial Organizations, Canadian Labour Congress.

Unit News 'Pay Gaps Widen' and OPEIU wage-comparison tables

newspaper-clipping content-sequence medium order high read 1987 2 pages

Subject: Post Unit News No. 99 story on widening pay gaps together with the second page of the OPEIU/AFL-CIO wage comparison.

Notes: Thematic pairing. Page 1 of the OPEIU report is in Martha 25.

p. 1 content 64358739-D03F-4BA7-9419-57F07EB33320_1_105_c.jpeg
Reporters1/869/87
White males$988.68$1,140.52
Black males$920.46$1,057.39
White females$859.37$1,008.56
Black females$791.33$ 899.33
Post Unit News
[logo: The Newspaper Guild AFL-CIO CLC]
Washington/Baltimore Newspaper Guild
No. 99 Date October 22, 1987
Discrimination at The Post
PAY GAPS WIDEN
The patterns of pay discrimination that have contributed over the years to low morale and wide gaps in the amount Washington Post managers are willing to pay their employees not only persist, but have widened significantly since management imposed its version of the contract in August.
If you needed a reason to understand why this contract is inadequate, here it is.
Last we looked, white males topped the salary scales in the best-paying jobs in many of the largest job categories at the Post. The news is this: despite the company's stated commitments to the hiring and nurturing of women and minorities, the practice of paying inequitably based on race and sex has not only continued--it's gotten worse.
For reporters--the largest single job category covered by the contract--the average salary for white males was $988.68 at the beginning of 1986. By September of 1987, that figure had jumped to $1,140.52.
Such a salary improvement is not the source of our complaints, however. The problem is that while white males were continuing to improve, females and blacks continued the slide away from parity.
The numbers compiled after the so-called impasse was declared in mid-August tell the story.
Across all classifications, white males average $332 more a week than black females, $204.39 more than white females and $230.66 more than black males.
Among reporters, the figures are reversed slightly, but the discrepancies remain just as stark. White male reporters, on the average, make $240.59 more a week than black females, a gap that is $43.24 wider than in January of 1986.
The gap has also grown by about $2 a week between white male and white female reporters and by about $15 a week between white male and black male reporters.
Among outside advertising salespersons, the gap also increased significantly. White males, on average, earned about $25 more a week than black males and black females in 1986, a disparity which has now increased to $96 weekly. White females in this classification earn on average $61.51 less than their male counterparts, up from a $43.03 average weekly difference in 1986.
And the gap among white men and white women who work as T-5 Data Programmers has opened dramatically, moving from an $8.33 average weekly difference in 1986 to an $86.07 average difference now.
There have been some improvements. Black females who are classified as managers, assistant managers, editors, deputy and associate editors have closed a more than $200 gap between their average salaries and salaries for white males.
But overall, the Post's record on equal pay for equal work sends a clear message to the rank and file at the Washington Post about which employees are valued at this company, and which are not.
These comparisons tell the story:
-over-

MARTHA M. HAMILTON
3615 Albemarle St. NW
Washington, D.C. 20008
202 362 1434
Current position: Financial news reporter with The Washington Post. Beats have included trade, business and the environment, taxes, the airline industry, the Washington area economy, the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, oil and energy. Previously a Metro reporter with the Post. 1972 -- 1979.
1971-1972 -- Co-director of the Agribusiness Accountabilty Project, a foundation-funded organization providing research and advocacy on behalf of family farmers. Author of The Great American Grain Robbery, a book about the international grain trade.
Other jobs: Reporter with The Newark News; freelance reporter in Buenos Aires, Argentina; reporter in the Washington bureau of The New York Journal of Commerce, covering agriculture, oil and energy and the Supreme Court, and assistant to the dean of the Lyndon B. Johnson School of Public Affairs in Austin, Texas.
Honors: 1977 National Endowment for the Humanities fellowship; 1978 Newspaper Guild Front Page award for investigative series on a D.C. real estate developer; 1986 Washington Post reporter-in-residence fellow, Duke University; 1994 National Press Foundation fellowship for environmental reporting; 1994 Associated Press Mark Twain award; Eisenhower Exchange Fellowshp to Taiwan, Oct. 1995.
Education: BA in history, with a minor in classics from The University of Texas.
Languages: Spanish and some Portuguese
Personal: Born in Houston, Texas. One daughter, Sarah Hamilton.

POST GUILD BULLETIN
[handwritten: 30]
Washington/Baltimore Newspaper Guild
Local 35 No. 23 Date Nov. 23, 1993
Your Guild supports you. Support your Guild!
Creating A Fair, Diverse Newsroom: The Missing Chapter
You may have seen the report by the Task Force on the Newsroom, which was headed by Mike Geller, assistant managing editor for foreign news. The report is a serious look at a problem that those of us in the newsroom have recognized for years: that our workplace has been moving too slowly to become a diverse workplace in which members of ethnic minority groups, women and gays feel equally welcomed, valued and challenged. The Task Force makes substantial recommendations that, if implemented, could go a long way toward creating a more diverse, civilized and productive workplace. The Guild leadership thinks that, if you care about these issues, you have a responsibility to take the couple of hours necessary to digest the report. We think there should be wide-ranging discussion of the report and its implications.
The Post Still Pays Black and Women Employees Less
The Post's difficulty in keeping and developing good reporters of all ethnic backgrounds and both genders has an important root that was not within the scope of the Task Force. The Post continues to pay women and minorities much less, across the board, than it does white men.
In 1991, the last year for which the Guild has salary data, our black reporters earned, on average, $6,299 less than our white reporters. Women earned $10,355 less than men. Our 1990 data, which have been further broken down, show similar gaps even when the reporters being compared have the same length of service with the Post. While two individual reporters' salaries might legitimately differ because of their relative performance, that could not be the case between racial groups or the sexes.
For years, the Guild has researched and publicized these gaps and others -- involving Hispanics, older employees and commercial departments as well as the newsroom. Since 1988, the Guild has had a complaint -- alleging pay discrimination based on sex, race and age -- at the D.C. Office of Human Rights.
Now, since newsroom management seems intent on dealing with the issues raised by the Task Force, we all may have an opportunity to reconsider the salary gaps, which effectively form a "missing chapter" in the discussion of why The Washington Post newsroom is not seen as a workplace that treats us all equally. The Guild will, in coming days, publish a more detailed report on the salary gaps to promote that discussion.
The Road Behind . . . And Ahead
Both the Guild and newsroom management have wrestled with the broad issue here for years -- with too little to show for it. The goals of the 1986 Hoagland commission on the newsroom are unfulfilled, as are those of the Guild's pay discrimination complaint. In the past few years, two rounds of settlement talks on the Guild's complaint have failed. But we think that two groups of people working toward the same goal -- a diverse and humane workplace -- ought to be able to do so together.
-- Jim Rupert
Unit Chair

Ashley's file: Average Salaries Show Patterns of Discrimintion.jpeg
AVERAGE SALARIES SHOW PATTERNS OF DISCRIMINATION
The pattern of discrimination is clearest in the "key minimum" positions, which cover 559 fulltime employees. The "key minimum" positions are those that pay the highest salary under the contract (except for positions that carry percentage differentials on top of salary). The current "key minimum"-- or top rate of pay -- for the positions listed below is $664.35. Few people are paid at that rate which has allowed the Post a free hand to set salaries.
MANAGERS; ASSISTANT MANAGERS; EDITORS; DEPVTY and ASSOCIATE EDITORS
White males: $938.71 Black males: $762.64
White females: * $880.47 Black females: $626.66
* Although white females in this category appear to be doing well there are only 10 of them compared with 42 white males. The top 10 white males in this category average $1081.99 in pay.
ASSISTANT EDITORS, ASSIGNMENT EDITORS, MAKEUP EDITORS
White males: $977.39 ** All blacks: $930.22
White females: $893.71
** When categories such as black male or black female are very small they have been consolidated because listing them separately would be too revealing about individual salaries. In some cases one of those categories may have a salary average that is higher than the average for white males. This is not an attempt to disguise that fact. In no case does the consolidated category with the higher salary contain more than 5 employees. So a high salary for one individual may produce a misleadingly high average salary for that small group.
COPY EDITORS
White males: $861.89 *** All others: $749.63
*** At the time the Post supplied the salary data there were only two black copy editors so that salary data has been consolidated with data for white females in order not to reveal information about individual salaries.
